> til sengen — Kristin var saa træt at hun orket neppe
> sætte sig imot det, slik som hun skulde for sømmelig
> hets skyld. Hun lot sig ta og leie ut av loftet av fru
> Aashild og Gyrid fra Skog. Brudesvendene stod ved:
> foten av trappen med brændende kjerter og dragne
> sverd; de slog ring om kvindeskaren og fulgte Kristin
> over tunet, op i gamleloftet.
> Konerne klædde av hende brudestasen, stykke for
> stykke, la det bort. Kristin saa at over senge
> foten hang den violblaa fløilskjolen som hun skulde
> bære imorgen, og oppåa laa et langt snehvitt, fintfoldet
> linklæde. Det var hustrulinet som Erlend hadde bragt
> med til hende; imorgen skulde hun binde op sit håar i
> knute og fæste det over. Det saa saa friskt og svalt
> og rogivende ut.
> Sidst stod hun foran brudesengen, paa nøkne føtter,
> bararmet, klædt alene i den ankelside, guldgule silke
> serk. Kronen hadde de sat paa hende igjen — den
> skulde brudgommen ta av hende, nåar de to blev alene.
> Ragnfrid la sine hænder paa datterens skuldre, kys
> set hendes kind — morens ansigt og hænder var under
> lig kolde, men det var som graat sprængte paa dypt
> inde i hendes bryst. Saa slog hun sengen op og bød
> bruden sætte sig ned i den. Kristin adlød og lænet sig
> tilbake mot hodegjerdets opstablede silkeputer — hodet
> maatte hun lute litt frem for at holde paa kronen. Fru
> Aashild dækket over hende til midjen, la hendes hænder
> paa silkespredet og tok i brudens skinnende håar, slog
> det frem over barmen og de nøkne, slanke over
> arme.
> Mændene førte brudgommen ind i loftet. Munan
> Baardsøn spændte guldbeltet med sverdet av Erlend
> — da han hængte det ind paa væggen over sengen,
> hvisket han noget til bruden — Kristin skjønte ikke
> hvad han sa, men hun smilte saa godt hun kunde.
> Brudesvendene snørte op Erlends silkeklædning og
> løftet det tunge, side plagg over hans hode. Han satte
> sig i karmstolen og de hjalp ham av med sporer og
> støvler.
> En eneste gang vaaget bruden sig til at se op og
> møte hans øine.
> Saa begyndte godnatbydningen. Loftet tømtes for
> bryllupsgjester. Sidst gik Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn ut og
> lukket døren til brudehuset.
> Erlend stod op, krænget av sig underklærne og
> slængte dem bort i bænken. Han stod foran sengen,
> tok kronen og silkebindlerne av hendes håar og la det
> bort paa bordet. Saa kom han tilbake og steg op i
> sengen. Og knælende paa leiet ved hendes side fattet
> han om hendes hode, klemte det ind til sit nøkne, hete
> bryst, mens han kysset panden efter hele den røde
> stripe som kronen hadde gjort.
> Hun kastet armene om hans skuldre og hulket høit —
> søtt og vildt følte hun, at nu kvarv det bort, rædselen,
> spøkelsessynerne — nu, nu igjen var det bare ham og
> hende. Han løftet hendes ansigt et øieblik, saa ned i
> det og strøk en haand over hendes ansigt og hendes
> legeme, underlig hastig og raat, som han rev væk et
> dække:
> «Glem,» bad han hviskende og hett, «glem alt, Kri
> stin min — alt uten det, at du er min egen hustru, og
> jeg er din egen husbond —»
> Med haanden slog han ut den sidste flammen og
> kastet sig ned hos hende i mørket, han hulket
> han og:
> «Jeg har aldrig trodd det, aldrig i disse aarene, at
> vi skulde leve denne dagen —»
> Utenfor i tunet tystnet staaket litt efter litt. Trætte
> av ridtet før paa dagen og øre av drik vimret gjesterne
> for skams skyld omkring en liten stund endda — men
> flere og flere sneg sig unna og fandt de steder, hvor
> de skulde sove.
> Ragnfrid fulgte dem som mest skulde hædres til
> pladserne deres og bød godnat. Husbonden, som skulde
> hjulpet hende med dette, saa hun ingen steder.
> Der stod bare nogen smaa flokker med ungdom —
> mest tjenestefolk — i det mørke tun, da hun tilslut sneg
> sig ut for at finde manden sin og faa ham med sig i
> seng. Lavrans var blit dygtig drukken ut paa kvelden,
> hadde hun set.
> Hun snublet over ham tilsidst, da hun listet letende
> utenfor bugaarden — han laa næsegrus i græsset bakom
> badstuen.
> Trevende i mørket kjendte hun — jo det var ham.
> Hun trodde han sov, tok i hans skulder og vilde ha
> ham op fra den iskolde bakken. Men han sov ikke —
> ikke helt ialfald.
> «Hvad vil du,» spurte han, grøtet i mælet.
> «Du kan ikke ligge her,» sa hustruen. Hun støttet
> ham, for han stod og sjanglet. Med den ene haanden
> børstet hun av ham nedover fløielsklædningen. «Det
> er tid vi gaar iseng vi og, husbond.» Hun tok ham
> under armen og leiet den ravende mand opover mot
> gaarden; de gik paa baksiden av bugaardsbygnin
> gerne.
> «Du saa ikke op du, Ragnfrid, da du sat i brude
> seng under kronen,» sa han som før. «Datteren vor,
> hiin var mindre blyg hun, hun var ikke blyg i øinene,
> da hun saa paa brudgommen sin.»
> «Hun har biet paa ham i syv halvaar,» sa moren
> sagte. «Da maatte hun vel turde se op —»
> «Nei saa djævelen om de har biet,» skrek faren, og
> hustruen tysset ræd paa ham.
> De var i den smale gaten mellem baksiden av skithuset
> og en skigar. Lavrans hug den knyttede haand i under
> stokken til avtrædet.
> «Jeg la dig her til spot og skam, du stok. Jeg la dig
> her for at skarnet kunde æte dig op. Jeg la dig her til
> straf for du slog ned den lille væne møen min. jeg
> skulde lagt dig over loftsdøren min og æret og takket
> dig med prydelig skurd for du berget hende fra skam
> og fra sorg — for du voldte at Ulvhild min døde et
> menløst barn —»
> Han vendte sig om, ravet indimot skigaren og faldt
> fremover den, med hodet ned paa armene graat han
> übændig, med lange, dype støn indimellem.
> Hustruen tok om hans skuldre:
> «Lavrans, Lavrans.» Men hun kunde ikke stagge
> ham. «Husbond.»
> «Aa aldrig, aldrig, aldrig skulde jeg git hende til
> den manden. Gud hjelpe mig — jeg har vel visst det
> hele tiden — han hadde brutt ned hendes ungdom og
> hendes fagre hæder. Jeg trodde det ikke, nei skulde
> jeg tro slik om Kristin — men jeg visste det endda.
> Endda saa er hun for god til den veke drengen som
> har ødt sig selv og hende — hadde han lokket hende
> ti ganger, jeg skulde ikke git hende til ham, saa han
> kan spilde mere av livet hendes og lykken —»
> «Hvad anden raad var der da,» sa moren opgit.
> «Du skjønner det jo du og, hun var alt hans —»
> «Ja jeg hadde ikke trængt at gjøre saa stort opstyr
> for at gi til Erlend det han alt hadde tat sig til selv,»
> sa Lavrans. «Det er en gjæv husbond hun har faat,
> Kristin min —.» Han rev i skigaren. Saa graat han
> igjen. Ragnfrid syntes, han var likesom blit litt mere
> ædru, men nu tok rusen overhaand igjen.
> Saa drukken og saa over sig fortvilet som manden
> var, syntes hun ikke, hun kunde dra ham op i aare
> stuen, der de skulde sove — der laa fuldt av gjester.
> Hun saa sig om — like ved var en liten laave, hvor de
> gjemte det gode høiet til hestene i vaaronnen. Hun gik
> bort og kek ind — der laa ingen; da tok hun og leiet
> manden ditind, lukket døren efter dem.
> Ragnfrid tok og karret høi op om sig og ham og
> lunet utenpaa med kapperne. Lavrans graat fra tid til
> anden og sa noget, men det var saa røret, at hun kunde
> ikke finde mening i det. Om en stund lettet hun hans
> hode op i fanget sit.
> «Kjære husbond min — nåar de nu har lagt slik en
> elskov til hinanden — kanhænde blir alt bedre end vi
> tror —»
> Lavrans svarte støtvis — syntes nu igjen klarere:
> «Skjønner du ikke — han har nu aldeles magten
> over hende, han som aldrig har kunnet raade sig selv.
> Vanskelig vil hun faa mot til at sætte sig imot noget
> hendes husbond vil — og maa hun det en dag, da vil
> hun pines saa bittert ved det selv — det milde barnet
> mit —.
> — Nu skjønner jeg snart ikke mere, hvorfor Gud
> lægger saa mange og tunge sorger paa mig — jeg har
> strævet trofast for at gjøre hans vilje. Hvorfor har han
> tat fra os børnene vore, Ragnfrid, ett efter ett — først
> sønnerne vore, saa liten Ulvhild, og nu har jeg git hende
> som jeg hadde kjærest, uten hæder, til en utryg og
> uforstandig mand. Nu har vi bare den lille tilbake
> >
> og det tykkes mig uklokt nu at glæde mig over hende,
> før jeg ser hvordan det gaar med hende, Ramborg.»
> Ragnfrid skalv som et løv. Da tok manden om hendes
> skulder:
> «Læg dig ned,» bad han, «saa sover vi —» og med
> hodet mot hustruens arm laa han en stund, sukket av
> og til, men tilslut sovnet han.
> Det var kulmørkt i laaven endda, da Ragnfrid rørte
> paa sig — hun undret sig over at hun hadde sovet.
> Hun følte ut for sig med haanden; Lavrans sat op med
> hænderne flettet om knærne.
> «Er du vaaken alt,» spurte hun forundret. «Fryser
> du?»
> «Nei,» svarte han rusten i mælet, «men jeg kan ikke
> sovne igjen.»
> «Er det Kristin du tænker paa,» spurte moren. «Det
> kan da gaa bedre end vi tror, Lavrans,» sa hun igjen.
> «Ja det er det jeg tænker paa,» sa manden. «Jaja.
> Mø eller kone, hun kom da i brudeseng med den hun
> hadde lagt sin elsk til. Det gjorde hverken du eller
> jeg, min stakkars Ragnfrid.»
> Hustruen gav et dumpt, dypt støn — hun slængte sig
> ned paa siden i høiet. Lavrans flyttet en haand bort
> paa hendes skulder.
> «Men jeg har ikke kunnet,» sa han heftig og vaande
> fuldt. «Nei jeg kunde ikke — være slik som du vilde
> ha mig — da vi var unge. Jeg er ikke slik —»
> Om en stund sa Ragnfrid sagte og i graat:
> «Vi har jo levet godt sammen allikevel — Lavrans —
> alle disse aarene.»
> «Saa har jeg trodd selv,» svarte han mørkt.
> r
> Tankerne tumlet og trængte sig i ham. Det ene
> nøkne blikket som brudgom og brud hadde slaat mot
> hinanden i, de to unge aasyn som flammet op i rød
> lue — ham tyktes det være en skamløshet. Det hadde
> svidd ham, dette var hans datter. Men han blev ved
> at se de øinene — og vildt og blindt stred han imot, at
> dækket skulde bli revet fra noget i hans eget hjerte,
> han hadde aldrig villet vite, at det var der, han hadde
> verget sig mot sin egen hustru, da hun lette efter det —.
> Han hadde ikke kunnet, tok han haardt i for sig selv.
> I djævelens navn, han var blit giftet bort som gut, han
> hadde ikke valgt selv, hun var ældre end ham — han
> hadde ikke attraaet hende; han hadde ikke villet lære
> dette av hende — elskov. Han blev het av blu endda,
> nåar han tænkte paa det — at hun vilde hat ham til
> at elske hende, nåar han ikke hadde villet en slik kjær
> lighet av hende. At hun hadde budt ham alt dette som
> han aldrig hadde bedt om.
> Han hadde været hende en god husbond, det hadde
> han trodd selv. Han hadde vist hende al den ære han
> kunde, git hende fuld myndighet med sig og raadspurt
> hende i alt, været hende tro — og seks børn hadde de
> da hat. Han hadde bare villet faa lov til at leve med
> hende uten hun altid skulde gripe efter dette i hans
> hjerte som han selv ikke vilde blotte —.
> Ingen hadde han baaret elskov til. — Ingunn, Karls
> hustru paa Bru. Lavrans rødmet i kulmørket. Han
> hadde altid gjestet dem, nåar han reiste ned gjennem
> dalen. Han hadde vist end ikke talt med husfruen i
> enerum en gang. Men nåar han saa hende — bare han
> tænkte paa hende, sanset han noget som den første lugt
> av jorderne om vaaren, straks sneen er gaat bort. Han
> visste det nu, det kunde hændt ham ogsaa — han
> kunde ha elsket han og.
> Men han var blit gift saa ung, og han var blit sky.
> Saa var han kommet til at trives bedst ute i ødeskogen
> — indpaa vidden, hvor alle liv som lever vil ha vidt
> rum omkring sig, rum til at flygte gjennem, — sky
> speider de mot hver fremmed som vil snige sig ind
> paa dem —.
> En tid paa aaret var der da dyrene i skogen og paa
> tjeldet glemte skyheten sin. Da de bruste mot hunnen
> sin. Men han hadde faat sin til gave. Og hun hadde
> budt ham alt han ikke hadde beilet om —.
> Men ungerne i reden. — De hadde været den lille lune
> flekken i ødet, den inderste søteste lyst i hans liv. De
> smaa lyse pikehoderne under hans haand. —
> Gift, det var han blit — uspurt næsten. Venner —
> han hadde mange, og han hadde ingen. Krig — det
> hadde været glæde, men der blev ikke mere krig —
> hans rustning hang oppe i loftet, litet brukt. Han var
> blit bonde —. Men han hadde hat døttrene — alt han
> hadde levet var blit kjært, fordi han trygget dem med
> det, de fine bløte smaa livene han hadde holdt i hæn
> derne sine. Han husket Kristins lille toaars skrot paa
> skuldren sin, hendes linbleke, myke håar mot kinden
> sin. Hendes smaahænder som holdt i hans belte mens
> hun stanget sin runde, haarde barnepande mot hans
> skulderblade, nåar han red ut med hende bak sig paa
> hesten.
> Og nu hadde hun de samme hete øinene — og hun
> hadde faat sit. Hun sat der i halvskyggen op mot sen
> gens silkeputer. I kjerteskjæret var hun helt gylden —
> gylden krone og gylden serk og gyldent håar utover
> de nøkne gyldne arme. Hendes øine var ikke blyge
> mere —.
> Faren kved av skam.
> Og endda var det som hans hjerte sprang i blødning.
> For det han ikke selv hadde faat. Og for hustruen her
> ved sin side som han ikke hadde kunnet gi.
> Syk av medynk tok han i mørket efter Ragnfrids
> haand:
> «Ja jeg trodde vi levet godt sammen,» sa han. «Jeg
> trodde du sørget over børnene vore. — Ja og at du
> hadde et tungt sind. Aldrig tænkte jeg paa det, at det
> kunde være mig som ikke var dig en god husbond —»
> Ragnfrid skalv som i krampe:
> «Du var en god husbond altid, Lavrans.»
> «Hm.» Lavrans sat med haken helt ned paa knærne.
> «Endda hadde du kanske faat bedre om du var blit
> gift slik som datter vor idag —»
> Ragnfrid for op, skrek lavt og skjærende:
> «Vet du! Hvordan fik du vite — hvor længe har du
> visst —»
> «Jeg vet ikke hvad du taler om,» sa Lavrans om en
> stund, med en underlig knuget stemme.
> «Jeg taler om det, at jeg var ikke mø, da jeg blev
> din hustru,» svarte Ragnfrid, og hendes røst var klar
> og klinger av fortvilelse.
> Om en stund svarte Lavrans som før:
> «Det har jeg aldrig visst før nu.»
> Ragnfrid laa ned i høiet, skaket av graat. Da rien
> var over, løftet hun hodet litt. Der begyndte at sive litt
> graat lys ind gjennem gluggen i væggen. Hun kunde
> skimte sin mand, at han sat med armene slynget om
> knærne, urørlig som han var av sten.
> «Lavrans — tal til mig —» jamret hun.
> «Hvad vil du jeg skal si,» spurte han übevægelig.
> «Aa jeg vet ikke — at du skulde bande mig — slaa
> mig —»
> «Det vilde være noget sent nu,» svarte manden; der
> var som skyggen av et haansmil i hans stemme.
> Ragnfrid graat igjen. «Ja jeg agtet ikke paa det, at
> jeg sveg dig. Jeg syntes, saa sveget og saa krænket
> var jeg blit selv. Der hadde ingen spart mig. De kom
> med dig — jeg saa dig jo bare tre ganger før vi blev
> gifte — jeg syntes du var bare en gut, rød og hvit —
> saa ung og barnslig —»
> «Jeg var det,» sa Lavrans, og der kom litt mere
> klang i stemmen hans. «Og derfor vilde jeg trodd, at
> du som var kvinde, du vilde være mere ræd for at —
> for at svige en som var saa ung, at han intet skjønte —»
> «Slik kom jeg til at tænke siden,» sa Ragnfrid graa
> tende. «Da jeg var blit kjendt med dig. Snart nok
> kom den tiden, da jeg vilde git sjælen min tyve ganger
> om jeg kunde blit skyldfri mot dig.»
> Lavrans sat taus og urørlig; da sa hustruen igjen:
> «Du spørger ikke om noget?»
> «Hvad skulde det nytte til. Det var ham som — vi
> møtte likfærden hans paa Feginsbrekka, da vi bar Ulv
> hild ind til Nidaros —»
> «Ja,» sa Ragnfrid. «Vi maatte gaa ut av veien —
> ind paa engen. Jeg saa dem føre baaren hans forbi —
> med prester og munke og væbnede svende. Jeg hørte
> han hadde faat en god død — forlikt sig med Gud.
> Jeg bad, der vi stod med Ulvhilds baare mellem os, at
> min synd og min sorg maatte lægges for hans føtter
> paa den sidste dag —»
> «ja du gjorde vel det,» sa Lavrans, og der var den
> samme skyggen av haan i hans stille stemme.
> «Du vet ikke alt,» sa Ragnfrid, kold av fortvilelse.
> «Mindes du, at han kom ut til os paa Skog den første
> vintren vi var gifte —»
> «Ja,» svarte manden.
> «Da Bjørgulv droges med døden —. Aa der hadde
> ingen spart mig. Han var drukken, da han gjorde det
> mot mig — siden sa han, han hadde aldrig hat mig
> kjær, han vilde ikke ha mig — han bad mig glemme
> det —. Far min visste ikke dette, han har ikke sveget
> dig, det maa du aldrig tro. Men Trond — vi var hin
> andens kjæreste venner dengangen, jeg klaget det for
> ham. Han vilde true den manden til at egte mig —
> men han var bare gutten, han fik bank—. Siden raadet
> han mig til at tie med det og ta dig —»
> Hun sat stille litt.
> «Da han kom ut til Skog —. Der var gaat et aar;
> jeg tænkte ikke saa meget paa det. Men han kom
> ditut — han sa, at han angret, han vilde hat mig nu,
> om jeg hadde været ugift — han holdt av mig. Saa
> sa han. Gud maa dømme om han sa sandt. Da han
> var reist — jeg turde ikke gaa paa fjorden, jeg turde
> ikke for synden, ikke med barnet. Og saa hadde jeg —
> saa hadde jeg begyndt at holde slik av dig!» Hun
> skrek engang, som i vildeste pine. Manden vendte fort
> hodet mot hende.
> «Da Bjørgulv var født — aa jeg syntes jeg hadde ham
> kjærere end livet mit. Da han laa og droges med døden
> — jeg tænkte, slukner han, saa slukner jeg og. Men
> jeg bad ikke Gud spare guttens liv —»
> Lavrans sat meget længe før han spurte, dødt og tungt:
> «Var det for jeg var ikke far hans?»
> «Jeg visste ikke om du var det,» sa Ragnfrid stiv
> nende.
> Længe sat de dødsens stille begge to. Da spurte
> manden med ett heftig:
> «Jesu navn, Ragnfrid — hvorfor sier du mig dette
> — nu?»
> «Aa jeg vet ikke.» Hun vred sine hænder saa finger
> leddene knaket. «For at du kan faa hevn paa mig.
> Jag mig ut av gaarden din —»
> «Tænker du det skulde hjelpe mig —.» Hans stemme
> skalv av haan. «Saa er der døttrene vore,» sa han stille.
> «Kristin — og den lille.»
> Ragnfrid sat en stund.
> «Jeg mindes hvordan du dømte om Erlend Nikulaus
> søn,» sa hun sagte. «Hvad dømmer du da om mig —»
> Der gik en lang kuldegysning gjennem mandens
> legeme — løste ut litt av stivheten.
> «Du har nu — vi har nu levet sammen i syv og tyve
> aar — snart. Det er ikke slik som med en fremmed
> mand. Jeg skjønner det, at du har hat det værre end
> vondt.»
> Ragnfrid sank hulkende sammen under hans ord.
> Hun vaaget sig til at ta bort paa hans ene haand. Han
> rørte sig ikke, sat stille som en død. Da graat hun
> høiere og høiere — hendes husbond sat like übevæge
> lig og saa mot den gråa lysning omkring døren. Til
> slut laa hun som om al graat var rundet ut av hende.
> Da strøk han flygtig nedover hendes arm. Saa tok
> hun paa at graate igjen.
> «Mindes du,» sa hun indimellem graaten. «Den man
> den som kom til os engang, mens vi var paa Skog.
> 24 — Undset: Kristin.
> Han som kunde de gamle kvadene? Mindes du ett om
> en død mand som var kommet tilbake fra kvalverdenen
> og sa sønnen sin frasagn om det han hadde set? Da
> hørtes gny fra helvedes dypeste grund, til sine mænd
> malte utro kvinder muld for mat. Blodige var stenene
> de drog, blodige hang hjerterne ut av deres bryst —»
> Lavrans sa intet.
> «Jeg har tænkt i alle disse aarene paa de ord,» sa
> Ragnfrid. «Hver dag var det som hjertet mit blødde,
> for hver dag syntes jeg, at jeg malte dig muld til’ mat —»
> Lavrans visste ikke selv, hvorfor han svarte som han
> gjorde. Han syntes han var tom og hul i brystet som en
> mand, de har ristet blodørn paa ryggen. Men han Ia
> haanden tungt og træt paa sin hustrus hode og mælte:
> «Muld maa der vel males, Ragnfrid min, før maten
> kan gro.»
> Da hun vilde ta hans haand og kysse den, rev han
> den heftig til sig. Saa saa han ned paa hustruen, tok
> hendes ene haand og la den paa sit knæ, lutet sit kolde
> og stivnede ansigt ned imot den. Og slik blev de sit
> tende, uten at røre sig og uten at tale mere sammen.


## English translation supplied to the annotator

> Lavrans and his guests were therefore in good spirits when they went down to greet the almsfolk in the hearth-room.
> The poor sat around the table, eating milk porridge and drinking good ale with it, and Kristin went about the table, serving the old and the sick.
> Lavrans greeted his guests and asked whether they were satisfied with the fare. Then he went over and welcomed an old pauper who had been moved to Jørundgaard that day. The man was named Haakon; he had been a warrior under old King Haakon and had accompanied the king on his last journey to Scotland. He was now utterly destitute and almost blind. People had offered to settle him in a cottage, but he preferred to be taken from farm to farm, for everywhere he was received more as an honored guest, because he possessed unusually great knowledge and had seen much of the world.
> Lavrans stood with his hand on his brother’s shoulder—Aasmund Bjørgulfsøn had come to Jørundgaard as a guest. He too asked Haakon how pleased he was with the food.
> “The ale is good, Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn,” said Haakon. “But a mare must have cooked our porridge today. ‘A bedfellow cook makes scorched porridge,’ says the proverb, and scorched this porridge is.”
> “That was unfortunate,” said Lavrans, “that I should give you scorched porridge. But the old proverb does not always hold true, I hope, for it was my daughter who cooked the porridge herself.” He laughed and bade Kristin and Tordis hurry and bring in the meat dishes.
> Kristin slipped quickly out and across toward the cookhouse. Her heart was pounding—she had caught a glimpse of her uncle’s face when Haakon said that about the cook and the porridge.
> Late that evening she saw her father and uncle walking up and down the yard for a long time, talking. She was dizzy with fear, and matters did not improve for her when, the next day, she noticed that her father was taciturn and unhappy. But he said nothing.
> Nor did he say anything after her uncle had left. But Kristin noticed that he did not speak as much with Haakon as he usually did, and when the time during which they were to keep the old man was over, Lavrans did not offer to take him for another term, but let him be moved to the next farm.
> Otherwise there were reasons enough why Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn might be unhappy and gloomy that summer, for it now looked unmistakably as though there would be a year of famine in the district, and the farmers convened an assembly to deliberate on how they should meet the approaching winter. By late summer it was already clear to most of them that they would have to slaughter or drive south for sale the greater part of their herds and buy grain to bring home as winter food for the people. The previous year had not been a good grain year, so the stores of old grain were smaller than usual.
> One morning early in the autumn, Ragnfrid went out with all three of her daughters to inspect some linen she had lying on the bleaching ground. Kristin praised her mother’s weaving highly. Then Ragnfrid stroked Ramborg’s hair:
> “This shall be for your chest, little one.”
> “Mother,” said Ulvhild, “shall I not have any chest, then, when I am to enter the convent?”
> “You know you will have no smaller dowry from home than your sisters,” said Ragnfrid. “But you will not need the same things. And you know that you are to stay with your father and me for as long as we live—if you wish it.”
> “And when you enter the convent,” said Kristin, uncertainty in her voice, “perhaps, Ulvhild, I shall already have been a nun for many years.”
> She looked over at her mother, but Ragnfrid was silent.
> “If I had been such that I could have married,” said Ulvhild, “then I should never have turned away from Simon—he was kind, and he was so sorrowful when he bade farewell to us all.”
> “You know your father has said that we are not to speak of this,” said Ragnfrid, but Kristin said defiantly:
> “Yes, I know he grieved more over parting from you than from me.”
> Her mother said angrily:
> “Then he would have had to possess little pride indeed if he had shown you that he grieved—you did not behave well toward Simon Andressøn, my daughter. And yet he begged us not to threaten you or curse you—”
> “No, he must have thought he had cursed me so much,” said Kristin as before, “that no one else needed to tell me how wretched I am. But I noticed no sign that Simon cared greatly for me before he understood that I held another man dearer than him.”
> “Go home, you two,” said the mother to the little ones. She sat down on a log that lay there and drew Kristin down beside her.
> “You surely know,” she began, “that it has always been held most seemly and honorable for a man not to speak too much of love to his betrothed—not to sit alone with her or show himself too ardent—”
> “Oh, I wonder,” said Kristin, “whether young people who love one another do not forget themselves once in a while, instead of always remembering what old people deem seemly.”
> “Take care, Kristin,” said her mother, “that you never forget it.” She sat for a while. “It is true enough, I understand, that your father fears you have bestowed your love upon a man to whom he would be loath to give you.”
> “What has my uncle said?” Kristin asked after a moment.
> “Nothing but this,” said her mother, “that Erlend of Husaby has a better lineage than reputation. Yes, he has evidently spoken to Aasmund and asked him to put in a word for him with Lavrans. Your father was not very pleased when he heard this.”
> But Kristin sat radiant. Erlend had spoken to her uncle. She who had suffered so because he had sent her no word.
> Then her mother said again:
> “Now it is also the case that Aasmund mentioned something about a report that is said to have circulated in Oslo: that this Erlend had been hanging about in the lanes around the convent and you had gone out and spoken with him by the fences.”
> “Well?” asked Kristin.
> “Aasmund advises this match, as you understand,” said Ragnfrid. “But then Lavrans grew angrier than I remember ever seeing him. He said that a suitor who took that road to his daughter would find him with sword in hand. Dishonorable enough was the way we had behaved toward the people of Dyfrin, but if Erlend had enticed you to run the roads with him in the dark, and while you were in a convent of nuns, then he took it as sufficient proof that you were better served by losing such a husband.”
> Kristin clenched her hands together in her lap—the color came and went in her face. Her mother put an arm around her waist—but Kristin twisted away and screamed, beside herself with rebellion:
> “Leave me alone, Mother! Perhaps you want to feel whether I have grown thicker around the waist—”
> The next moment she was standing, holding her hand to her cheek—bewildered, she looked down into her mother’s blazing face. No one had struck her since she was a little child.
> “Sit down,” said Ragnfrid. “Sit down,” she repeated, and her daughter obeyed. Her mother sat silent for a while; when she spoke, her voice was uncertain:
> “I have seen it, Kristin: you have never held me in much affection. I thought perhaps it was because you believed I did not love you so much—not as your father loves you. I let it pass—I thought that when the time came for you yourself to have borne a child, then you would surely understand—.
> “I was still nursing you, and even then it was already so that when Lavrans came near the two of us, you let my breast fall from your mouth, reached toward him, and laughed so that my milk ran over your lips. Lavrans thought this amusing—and God knows I did not begrudge him that; I did not begrudge you that your father played and laughed whenever he saw you. I myself thought it a pity for you, poor little life, that I could not cease all my weeping. I thought more about whether I should lose you too than I rejoiced that I had you. But God and the Virgin Mary know that I loved you no less than Lavrans did.”
> Tears ran down Ragnfrid’s cheeks, but her face was quite calm, and so was her voice:
> “God knows I never bore a grudge against him or against you for the friendship between you. I thought I had not given him too much joy in the years we had lived together; I was glad he had you. And I also thought that if Ivar, my father, had been like that toward me—.
> “There are many things, Kristin, that a mother should teach her daughter to guard against. I thought it was not needed with you, who have followed your father all these years—you should surely know what is honorable and right. What you mentioned just now—do you think I could have imagined that you would wish to cause Lavrans such grief—.
> “I only wanted to tell you—I wish that you may have a husband whom you can love. But then you must conduct yourself wisely—do not let Lavrans get the thought that you have chosen a man of misfortune, one who does not respect women’s peace or honor. For he will not give you to such a man—not even if it meant saving you from manifest shame. Lavrans would sooner let steel decide the right between himself and the man who had ruined your life—.”
> With that her mother rose and left her.
> 
> 11.
> 
> On Saint Bartholomew’s Day, the twenty-fourth of August, the blessed King Haakon’s daughter’s son was acclaimed at the Haugating. Among the men sent there from northern Gudbrandsdal was Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn. He had been called the king’s man since his youth, but in all these years he had seldom come near the court, and he had never tried to turn to his advantage the good name he had won in the campaign against Duke Erik. Nor was he eager to travel to the assembly of acclamation, but he could not excuse himself. The delegates from the northern valley were also charged with trying to buy grain in the south of the country and send it by ship to Raumsdal.
> The people in the districts were dispirited and afraid of the approaching winter. The farmers also thought it ill that once again a child should become king of Norway. Old people remembered the time when King Magnus had died and his sons were children, and Sira Eirik said:
> “Væ terræ, ubi puer rex est. In the Norwegian tongue that means: there is no rest at night for the rats on a farm where the cat is young.”
> Ragnfrid Ivarsdatter managed the farm while her husband was away, and it was good for both Kristin and her that their heads and hands were full of care and work. Throughout the district people struggled to gather moss in the mountains and strip bark, for little hay and almost no straw had been harvested, and even the foliage gathered after Saint John’s Mass had yellowed and was poor. On Holy Cross Day, when Sira Eirik carried the crucifix over the fields, many in the procession wept and loudly begged God to have mercy on man and beast.
> About a week after Holy Cross Day, Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn came home from the assembly.
> It was long past people’s bedtime, but Ragnfrid was still sitting in the weaving-room. She had so much to attend to during the days now that she often worked late into the evenings at weaving and sewing. Ragnfrid also liked this house very much. It was said to be the oldest room on the farm; they also called it the dyngje, and people said it had stood there since heathen times. Kristin and the maid named Astrid were with her, sitting and spinning by the hearth.
> They had sat silent and sleepy for some time when they heard the hoofbeats of a single horse—a man came riding at great speed into the wet yard. Astrid went to the vestibule and looked out; immediately afterward she returned, followed by Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn.
> Both his wife and daughter saw at once that he was rather drunk. He staggered and caught hold of the smoke-hole pole while Ragnfrid relieved him of his soaking-wet cloak and hat and unbuckled his sword belt.
> “What have you done with Halvdan and Kolbein?” she said, somewhat alarmed. “Did you ride away from them on the road?”
> “No, I rode away from them at Loptsgaard,” he said, laughing a little. “I had such a longing to come home—I get no peace before I do—they went to bed down there, but then I took Guldsveinen and made for home—”
> “You must find me a little food, Astrid,” he said to the maid. “Carry it in here, so you need not walk so far in the rain. But be quick; I have eaten nothing since early today—”
> “Did you get no food at Loptsgaard?” his wife asked in surprise.
> Lavrans sat on the bench, rocking himself and laughing a little:
> “You know there was food—but I had no appetite while I was there. I drank awhile with Sigurd—but—then I thought I might as well go home at once as wait until tomorrow—”
> Astrid came with ale and food; she had also brought dry shoes for the master.
> Lavrans fumbled at unfastening his spurs, but nearly fell forward.
> “Come here, Kristin,” he begged, “and help your father. I know you do it from a loving heart—a loving heart, yes—today.”
> Kristin obeyed and knelt down. Then he put both hands around her head and lifted it:
> “You surely know this, my daughter—I wish nothing but your good. I would not cause you one sorrow unless I saw that I was sparing you many sorrows afterward. You are still very young, Kristin—you turned seventeen this year—three days after Saint Hallvard’s Mass—seventeen years old you are—”
> Kristin had finished serving him. A little pale, she rose and sat again on her stool by the hearth.
> Lavrans’s drunkenness seemed to diminish somewhat as he ate his fill. He answered his wife’s and the serving-maid’s questions about the assembly—yes, it had been splendid. They had managed to buy grain and some flour and malt, some in Oslo and some in Tunsberg; they were foreign wares, could have been better, but could also have been worse. Yes, he had met many kinsmen and acquaintances and brought greetings home. But he sat there letting the answers drip from him.
> “I spoke with Sir Andres Gudmundsøn,” he said when Astrid had gone out. “Simon has drunk his betrothal ale with the young widow of Manvik. The wedding is to be held at Dyfrin around Saint Andrew’s Mass. The boy has governed the matter himself this time. I kept away from Sir Andres in Tunsberg, but he sought me out—wanted to tell me that he knew for certain Simon saw Lady Halfrid for the first time at midsummer this year. He feared I might think Simon had had this rich marriage in mind when he broke with us.” Lavrans sat a moment and laughed mirthlessly. “You understand, this honorable man was greatly afraid we might think such a thing of his son.”
> Kristin breathed more easily. She thought this was what had upset her father so much. Perhaps he had hoped all along that this marriage between Simon Andressøn and her might still come about. At first she had feared he had learned something about her conduct in Oslo.
> She rose and bade them good night. Then her father said she must wait a little longer.
> “I have one more piece of news,” said Lavrans. “I could have kept it from you, Kristin—but it is better that you know. It is this: the man upon whom you have set your heart—you must try to forget him.”
> Kristin had been standing with hanging arms and bowed head. Now she looked up into her father’s face. She moved her lips, but no audible sound came forth.
> Lavrans looked away from his daughter’s gaze; he flung out his hand:
> “You surely know that I would not oppose it if I could truly believe it would be for your good.”
> “What news have you heard on this journey, Father?” said Kristin, her voice clear.
> “Erlend Nikulaussøn and his kinsman Sir Munan Baardsøn came to me in Tunsberg,” answered Lavrans. “Sir Munan asked for you on Erlend’s behalf, and I answered no.”
> Kristin stood for a while, breathing heavily.
> “Why will you not give me to Erlend Nikulaussøn?” she asked.
> “I do not know how much you know about the man whom you wish to have for husband,” said Lavrans. “If you cannot understand the reason yourself, it will not be pleasant for you to hear it from my mouth.”
> “Is it because he has been excommunicated and outlawed?” asked Kristin as before.
> “Do you know what caused King Haakon to banish his near kinsman from his court—and that in the end he fell under the ban because he defied the archbishop’s command—and that he did not leave the country alone?”
> “Yes,” said Kristin. Her voice became uncertain. “I also know that he was eighteen years old when he became acquainted with her—his concubine.”
> “That old was I when I married,” answered Lavrans. “When I was young, we reckoned that at eighteen years of age a man could answer for himself and govern his own and others’ welfare.”
> Kristin stood silent.
> “You called that woman his concubine, with whom he lived for ten years and begot children,” said Lavrans after a moment. “Little joy should I have on the day I sent my daughter from home with a husband who had lived in open concubinage year after year before marrying. But you know that concubinage it was not.”
> “You did not judge Lady Aashild and Sir Bjørn so harshly,” said Kristin softly.
> “Even so, I cannot say I should gladly see us become allied by marriage with them,” answered Lavrans.
> “Father,” said Kristin, “have you then been so sinless all your life that you dare judge Erlend so harshly—”
> “God knows,” answered Lavrans sternly, “that I judge no man to be a greater sinner than I am myself. But one cannot reckon matters so that I must give away my daughter to any man who pleases to ask for her merely because we all need God’s mercy.”
> “You know I did not mean it so,” said Kristin passionately. “Father—Mother—you have been young—do you remember so little that you do not know it is not easy to guard oneself against the sin that love causes—”
> Lavrans became blood-red.
> “No,” he said shortly.
> “Then you do not know what you are doing,” Kristin screamed in despair, “if you separate Erlend Nikulaussøn and me!”
> Lavrans sat down again on the bench.
> “You are only seventeen, Kristin,” he began again. “It may be that he and you—that you have grown dearer to each other than I thought. But he is not so young a man that he should not have understood—had he been a good man, he would not have approached so young and legally dependent a child as you are with words of love. That you were promised to another must have seemed a small matter to him—.
> “But I will not marry my daughter to a man who has two children with another man’s lawful wife. Do you know that he has children?
> “You are too young to understand that such a wrong breeds strife—and discord in a lineage—without end. The man cannot betray his own offspring, nor can he set matters right—it will be hard to find a way to bring his son forward among people or marry his daughter to any man other than a servant or small farmer. Those children would have to be without flesh and blood if they did not come to hate you and your children—.
> “Do you not understand, Kristin—such sins—God may forgive such sins more readily than many others, but they ravage a lineage in a way that can never be remedied. I thought of Bjørn and Aashild too—there stood this Munan, her son, covered in gold; he sits in the council of the king’s counselors; he and his brothers have their maternal inheritance, and he has not greeted his mother in all these years of her poverty. Yes, that was the man your friend had chosen as his spokesman.
> “No, I say, no. Into that lineage you shall not enter while my head is above the earth.”
> Kristin covered her face with her hands and burst into tears:
> “Then I shall pray to God night and day, night and day, that if you do not change your mind, he may take me from here!”
> “It is useless to speak more of this tonight,” said her father, tormented. “You may not believe it, but I must govern you as I believe I can answer for it. Go to rest now, child.”
> He held out his hand to her, but she would not look at it and went sobbing out of the room.
> Her parents remained sitting for a while. Then Lavrans said to his wife:
> “Will you fetch me a drink of ale—no, bring in some wine,” he asked. “I am tired—.”
> Ragnfrid did as he asked. When she returned with the tall goblet, her husband sat with his face in his hands. He looked up, glanced over her headdress and down her arms:
> “Poor thing, now you are wet.—Drink to me, then, Ragnfrid.”
> She barely touched her mouth to the goblet.
> “No, drink with me now,” said Lavrans vehemently, and tried to pull his wife down onto his lap. Reluctantly she yielded to him. Lavrans said:
> “You will surely stand with me in this matter, my wife? It will be best for Kristin herself if she understands from the outset that she must put this man out of her mind.”
> “It will be hard for the child,” said her mother.
> “Yes, I understand that,” answered Lavrans.
> They sat for a while; then Ragnfrid asked:
> “What does he look like, this Erlend of Husaby?”
> “Oh,” said Lavrans, drawing out the word. “He is a handsome fellow—in a way. But he does not look fit for much else than beguiling women.”
> They were silent again for a while; then Lavrans said once more:
> “The great inheritance he received after Sir Nikulaus he has evidently managed so that it has greatly diminished. It was not for such a son-in-law that I labored and sought to secure my children.”
> The mother paced restlessly up and down the floor. Lavrans continued:
> “What I liked least was that he tried to entice Kolbein with silver—he was to carry a secret letter from him to Kristin.”
> “Did you look at the letter?” asked Ragnfrid.
> “No, I did not care to,” said Lavrans curtly. “I handed it back to Sir Munan and told him what I thought of such conduct. He had hung his seal from it too—I do not know what one is to say of such childish tricks. Sir Munan showed me the seal: it was King Skule’s privy seal, which Erlend possesses after his father. He must have thought I should remember that it is a great honor they are asking for my daughter. But I think Sir Munan would not have pursued this matter for Erlend with such warmth had it not been because he understands that with this man the power and honor of the Husaby lineage, which it gained in Sir Nikulaus’s and Sir Baard’s days, are declining—Erlend can no longer expect to make such a marriage as his birth entitled him to.”
> Ragnfrid stopped before her husband:
> “Now I do not know, husband, whether you are right in this matter. First it must be said that, times being as they are, many men on the great estates have had to content themselves with less power and honor than their fathers before them. You yourself know best that it is less easy for a man to gain wealth either from land or trade than it was formerly—”
> “I know, I know,” the man interrupted impatiently; “all the more important, then, to govern prudently what one has inherited—”
> But his wife continued:
> “There is also this to say: it does not seem to me that Kristin can be an unequal match for Erlend. In Sweden your lineage is among the best; your grandfather and your father bore the name of knight in this country. My forefathers were lendermen, son after father, for many hundreds of years down to old Ivar; my father and grandfather were royal governors. It is true that neither you nor Trond has received titles or land under the Crown. But then, it seems to me, one might say that Erlend Nikulaussøn stands no differently than you.”
> “It is not the same,” said Lavrans vehemently. “Power and knighthood lay ready to Erlend’s hand, and he turned away from them for adultery’s sake. But now I understand that you too are against me. Perhaps you think, like Aasmund and Trond, that it is an honor for me that these great lords wish to have my daughter for one of their kinsmen—”
> “I have told you,” said Ragnfrid somewhat heatedly, “that I do not think you need be so sensitive that you fear Erlend’s kinsmen will imagine they are lowering themselves in this matter. But do you not understand, above all else—the gentle, yielding child had the courage to rise against us and reject Simon Darre—have you not seen that Kristin has been entirely unlike herself since she came from Oslo? Do you not see that she walks as though she had come out from under the mountain—.
> “Do you not understand that she holds this man so dear that, if you do not yield, some great misfortune may happen here?”
> “What do you mean by that?” asked the father, looking sharply up.
> “Many a man greets his son-in-law without knowing it,” said Ragnfrid.
> It was as though the man stiffened; slowly his face turned white:
> “You who are her mother!” he said hoarsely. “Have you—have you seen—such certain signs—that you dare accuse your own daughter of this—”
> “No, no,” said Ragnfrid quickly. “I did not mean what you think. But no one can know what has happened or what may happen. She possesses no thought except that she loves this man—I have seen it—one day she might show us that she holds him dearer than honor—or life!”
> Lavrans sprang up:
> “Oh, you are mad! Can you think such things of our fair, good child! She cannot have had much opportunity where she was—with the nuns. She is not a cow-girl, surely, who lies down behind a rail fence. You can understand that she cannot have seen this man or spoken with him very many times—it will surely pass; it is only a young maiden’s fancy.
> “God knows it seems heavy enough to me to see her grieve so, but you must know that with time this must pass!
> “Life, you say, and honor—. Here at home on my own farm I can surely guard my maiden. Nor do I believe that any maiden of good lineage, reared in Christianity and chastity, parts so quickly with honor or life either. Hah, those are things people make ballads about—but I think it is so that when a man or maiden is tempted to do such a thing, they make a ballad about it and are helped thereby, but refrain from doing it—
> “You yourself,” he said, stopping before his wife. “There was another whom you would rather have had at the time the two of us were joined. What sort of lot do you think you would have had if your father had let you govern that matter—?”
> Now it was Ragnfrid who had turned deathly pale:
> “Jesus, Mary! Who told you—”
> “Sigurd of Loptsgaard said something about it—it was just after we had moved here to the valley,” said Lavrans. “But answer what I asked—do you think you would have been happier if Ivar had given you to that man?”
> His wife stood with her head deeply bowed.
> “That man,” she answered almost inaudibly, “did not want me.” A convulsion seemed to pass through her body—she struck out into the air with a clenched hand.
> Then her husband carefully laid his hands on her shoulders.
> “Is that it?” he asked, overwhelmed, and a deep, sorrowful wonder burst into his voice. “Is that it—all these years—have you borne sorrow for him—Ragnfrid?”
> She trembled violently, but said nothing.
> “Ragnfrid?” he asked as before. “But since Bjørgulf died—and afterward—when you—when you wished—I should be toward you—as I could not be. Were you thinking of the other then?” he whispered, frightened, confused, and tormented.
> “How can you conceive such thoughts?” she whispered through tears.
> Lavrans set his forehead against his wife’s and shook his head a little.
> “I do not know. You are so strange—everything you said tonight. I grew afraid, Ragnfrid. I suppose I do not understand a woman’s mind—”
> Ragnfrid smiled faintly and put her hands around his neck:
> “God knows, Lavrans—I begged you because I loved you more than is good for a human soul. And I hated the other so that I felt the devil rejoice in it.”
> “I have loved you, my wife,” said Lavrans softly and kissed her, “with all my heart. You know that? It seemed to me that we had lived well together—Ragnfrid?”
> “You were the best of husbands,” she said with a little sob, and hid herself against him.
> He clasped her passionately:
> “Tonight I should like to sleep with you, Ragnfrid. And if you would be toward me as in the old days, then I should not be—such a fool—”
> His wife stiffened in his arms and drew a little away:
> “It is a time of fasting now,” she said softly—with a strange hardness.
> “So it is.” The man laughed a little. “You and I, Ragnfrid, have kept every fast and tried to live by God’s commandments in all things. And now it almost seems to me—we might perhaps have been happier if we had had more to repent of.”
> “Do not speak so,” his wife begged despairingly, pressing her thin hands against his temples. “You know that I do not wish you to do anything except what you yourself think right.”
> He drew her to him once more—he groaned aloud as he did so:
> “God help her. God help us all, my Ragnfrid.
> “I am tired,” he said, releasing her. “You too will go to rest now, surely?”
> He stood by the door and waited while she extinguished the hearth, blew out the little iron lamp by the loom, and pinched out the spark. Together they went through the rain toward the dwelling-house.
> Lavrans already had his foot on the loft stair when he turned back to his wife, who still stood in the vestibule doorway. He drew her passionately to him one last time and kissed her in the darkness. Then he made the sign of the cross over his wife’s face and went upstairs.
> Ragnfrid flung off her clothes and crept into bed. For a while she lay listening to her husband’s steps overhead in the upper loft-room—then the bed creaked up there, and all became still. Ragnfrid pressed her slender arms over her withered breasts.
> Yes, God help me. What kind of woman was she, what kind of mother was she? Now she was almost old. And still she was the same. She no longer begged as when they were young, when she had stormed and implored this man who closed himself off, shy and bashful, when she grew ardent—who became cold when she wished to give him more than his marital right. So it had been—and so she had conceived child after child—humiliated, raging with shame because she could not content herself with his lukewarm husbandly love. Then, when she went about needing kindness and tenderness, he had had so much to give—the man’s tireless, gentle solicitude for her when she was ill and afflicted fell like dew over her burning mind. He willingly took up everything of hers and bore it—but there was something of his own that he would not give. She had loved her children so that it was as though her heart were torn out of her whenever she lost one—God, God, what kind of person was she, who amid her torments had been able to taste this drop of sweetness: that he took her sorrow into himself and laid it close against his own.
> Kristin—she would gladly have gone through fire for her daughter—they did not believe it, neither Lavrans nor the child, but it was so. Yet now she felt an anger resembling hatred toward her—it was to forget his sorrow over the child’s sorrow that he had wished tonight he could surrender himself to his wife.
> Ragnfrid dared not rise, for she did not know whether Kristin might be lying awake in the other bed. But she rose soundlessly to her knees, and with her forehead bowed against the footboard of the bed, she tried to pray. For her daughter, for her husband, and for herself. While the cold little by little stiffened her body, she set out once more upon one of her familiar night wanderings, trying to break a path to a peaceful home for her heart.
> 
> III.
> 
> Haugen lay high on the slope on the western side of the valley.
> On this moonlit night the whole world was white. Wave after wave of white mountain arched beneath the pale-blue, star-poor sky. Even the shadows cast by peaks and ridges across the snowfields seemed strangely light and bright, for the moon sailed so high.
> Down toward the valley the forest stood loosely white with snow and rime around the white expanses of fields, with their tiny tracery of fences and houses. But at the very bottom of the valley the shadows thickened into darkness.
> Lady Aashild came out of the cowshed, closed the door behind her, and stood for a while in the snow. The whole world white, and still more than three weeks remained before Advent began. Saint Clement’s Day cold—winter must have come in earnest. Ah yes, that usually went with a year of famine—.
> The old woman sighed heavily into the desolation. Winter again, and cold and loneliness. Then she picked up the milk pail and lantern and went toward the dwelling-house. She looked out once more.
> Four black specks came out of the forest halfway down the slope. Four mounted men—the tip of a spear flashed in the moonlight. Their ascent was laborious—no one had traveled here since the snowfall. Could they be coming here—.
> Four armed men—. It did not seem likely that anyone with lawful business with her would travel in such company. She thought of the chest containing her and Bjørn’s possessions. Should she hide in the outbuilding?
> She looked out over the winter and the desolation around her. Then she went into the room. The two old dogs that had been lying before the smoke-oven thumped their tails against the floorboards. Bjørn had taken the young dogs with him into the mountains.
> She blew upon the embers in the oven and put in wood, filled the iron pot with snow, and set it by the fire. She poured the milk into a wooden dish and carried it into the chamber behind the room.
> Aashild changed out of the dirty, undyed homespun gown that stank of cowshed and sweat, put on a dark-blue garment, and exchanged her coarse linen headcloth for a white linen cloth, which she arranged around her head and neck. She took off the fur-lined leather boots and put on silver-buckled shoes.
> Then she began to put her room in order—smoothed the cushions and skins in the bed after Bjørn had lain there during the day, wiped the long table, and straightened the bench cushions.
> Lady Aashild stood before the oven stirring the evening porridge when the dogs gave warning. She heard the horses in the yard, men entered the gallery, and someone struck the door with a spear. Aashild lifted the pot from the fire, adjusted her clothing, and, with the dogs beside her, went forward and opened the door.
> Out in the moonlit yard three young men held four frost-covered horses. The man standing in the gallery cried gladly:
> “Aunt Aashild, you come yourself to open the door! Then I must say, Bien trouvé!”
> “Nephew—why, is it you! Then I say the same to you! Go into the room while I show your men to the stable.”
> “Are you alone on the farm?” asked Erlend. He followed while she settled the men.
> “Yes, Sir Bjørn and our farmhand went in with the sledge—they were to see if they could bring home some fodder we gathered near the mountain,” said Lady Aashild. “And I have no serving-woman,” she said, laughing.
> A little later the four young men sat on the outer bench, their backs to the table, watching the old lady as she quietly and busily set out food for them.
> She spread a cloth on the table and set a lighted candle there, brought butter, cheese, a bear ham, and a tall stack of thin, fine flatbreads. She fetched ale and mead from the cellar beneath the room, then poured the porridge into a fine wooden dish and bade them sit down and begin.
> “It is little enough for you young fellows,” she said, laughing. “I must cook another pot of porridge. Tomorrow you shall fare better—but I close the cookhouse in winter except when I bake or brew. We are few people on this farm, and I am beginning to grow old, kinsman.”
> Erlend laughed and shook his head. He noticed that his men behaved toward the old woman with a courtesy and reverence such as he had never seen in them before.
> “You are a strange woman, Aunt. Mother was ten years younger than you are, and she looked older when we were last with you than you do tonight.”
> “Yes, youth passed swiftly enough from Magnhild,” said Lady Aashild softly. “Where have you come from now?” she asked a little later.
> “I have been for a while at a farm north in Lesja,” said Erlend. “I had hired lodging there. I do not know whether you can guess what business I have in these districts?”
> “You mean whether I know that you have had a suit made for the daughter of Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn down here at Jørundgaard?” said Lady Aashild.
> “Yes,” said Erlend. “I had her asked for in a seemly and honorable fashion, and Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn answered a flat no. Now I know no better counsel, since Kristin and I will not let ourselves be forced apart, than that I take her away by force. I have—I have had a watcher here in the district, and I know that her mother was to be at Sundbu from Saint Clement’s Day onward for a time, and Lavrans is out at the headland with the other men who are to fetch Sil’s winter supplies.”
> Lady Aashild sat for a while:
> “That course, Erlend, you had better abandon,” she said. “Nor do I believe the maiden will follow you willingly, and surely you will not use force.”
> “Yes, she will. We have spoken of this many times—she herself has begged me many times to carry her away.”
> “Has Kristin—!” said Lady Aashild. Then she laughed. “For that very reason you must not trust that you will get the maiden to go with you when you come to take her at her word.”
> “Yes,” said Erlend. “And now I had thought, Aunt, that you should send word to Jørundgaard asking whether Kristin might come and visit you—a week or so while her parents are away. Then we could be at Hamar before anyone notices she is gone,” he explained.
> Lady Aashild answered, still smiling slightly:
> “Have you also considered what Sir Bjørn and I are to answer when Lavrans comes and calls us to account for his daughter?”
> “Yes,” said Erlend. “We were four armed men, and the maiden was willing.”
> “I will not help you with this,” said the lady passionately. “Lavrans has been a faithful friend to us for many years; he and his wife are honorable people, and I will not lend myself to betraying them or dishonoring her. Leave that maiden in peace, Erlend. It is also high time your kinsmen heard of some deed from you other than that you roam in and out of the country with stolen women—”
> “We must speak together in private, Aunt,” said Erlend shortly.
> Lady Aashild took a candle, entered the chamber, and shut the door behind her. She sat on a flour bin; Erlend stood with his hands thrust into his belt and looked down at her.
> “You can also tell Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn that Sira Jon of Gerdarud married us before we traveled onward to Lady Ingebjørg Haakonsdatter in Sweden.”
> “So,” said Lady Aashild. “Do you know whether Lady Ingebjørg will receive you well when you come there?”
> “I spoke with her in Tunsberg,” said Erlend. “She greeted me as her dear kinsman and thanked me because I offered her my service here or in Sweden. And Munan has promised to give me letters to take to her.”
> “Then you surely know,” said Lady Aashild, “that even if you can get a priest to marry you, Kristin has forfeited all right to goods and inheritance after her father. And her children will not be your lawful heirs. It is uncertain whether she will be regarded as your wife.”
> “Perhaps not in this country. That is also why I seek refuge in Sweden. Her forefather, Laurentius the Lawman, was never married to Maiden Bengta in any other way—they never obtained her brother’s consent. Yet she was counted a lady—”
> “There were no children,” said Lady Aashild. “Do you think my sons will keep their hands from your inheritance if Kristin is left a widow with children and it can be disputed whether they were born legitimate?”
> “You wrong Munan,” said Erlend. “I know little of your other children—you have no reason to be kindly disposed toward them, I know. But Munan has always been my wholly faithful kinsman—he wants me married; he pleaded my suit with Lavrans. Otherwise, I can adopt into inheritance and lineage the children we have.”
> “By that you brand their mother a concubine,” said Lady Aashild. “But I do not understand how that meek man Jon Helgesøn will dare incur trouble with his bishop by marrying you against the law.”
> “I confessed to him this summer,” said Erlend quietly. “Then he promised to marry us if every other course should fail.”
> “So,” said Lady Aashild. “Then you have loaded a grievous sin upon yourself, Erlend. Kristin lived well at home with her father and mother—a good marriage with a handsome and honorable man of good lineage had been arranged for her.”
> “Kristin herself has said,” replied Erlend, “that you spoke of how she and I might suit each other well. And that Simon Andressøn was no husband for her.”
> “Oh, I have said this and I have said that,” his aunt snapped. “I have said a great deal in my time. Nor do I understand how you could have gained your will with Kristin so easily. You cannot have met many times. And I would not have thought that maiden easy to win—.”
> “We met in Oslo,” said Erlend. “Afterward she stayed with her uncle in Gerdarud. She came out and met me in the forest.” He looked down and said very softly: “I had her there alone to myself—.”
> Lady Aashild sprang up. Erlend bowed his head still lower.
> “And after that she was—was friendly with you?” the lady asked incredulously.
> “Yes.” Erlend smiled weakly and tremulously. “We were friends after that. And it was not so greatly against her will—but she is without guilt. It was then she wanted me to take her away—she would not return to her kinsmen—”
> “But you would not?”
> “No, I wished to try to gain her as wife with her father’s consent.”
> “Was this long ago?” asked Lady Aashild.
> “It was a year ago around Saint Lawrence’s Mass,” answered Erlend.
> “You have not been hasty in making your suit,” said the other.
> “She had not been released from the former betrothal,” answered Erlend.
> “And since then you have not come too near her?” asked Aashild.
> “We arranged matters so that we met a few times”—again the trembling smile passed over the man’s face—“at a house in the town.”
> “In God’s name,” said Lady Aashild. “I shall help you and her as much as I can. I understand it must be too hard for Kristin to walk there among her parents with such a thing to bear. There is no more, surely?” she asked.
> “Not that I have heard,” said Erlend shortly.
> “Have you considered,” the lady asked after a while, “that Kristin has friends and kinsmen down through the valley?”
> “We must travel secretly as much as we can,” said Erlend. “Therefore it is important that we depart swiftly, so that we are some distance away before her father comes home. You must lend us your sledge, Aunt.”
> Aashild shrugged her shoulders:
> “Then there is her uncle at Skog—what if he hears that you are celebrating your wedding with his brother’s daughter in Gerdarud?”
> “Aasmund has spoken for me to Lavrans,” said Erlend. “He cannot be privy to it, that is true, but he will surely shut one eye—we must reach the priest by night and travel onward by night. Then I think Aasmund will afterward urge upon Lavrans that it does not befit a God-fearing man such as he to separate us once we have been married by a priest—he must rather give his consent so that we become lawful spouses. You must say the same to the man. He may set whatever conditions he himself wishes for reconciliation between us and demand such amends as he thinks reasonable.”
> “I do not think Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn will be easy to counsel in this matter,” said Lady Aashild. “God and Saint Olav know that I greatly dislike this undertaking, nephew. But I understand it is the last course you can take if you are to remedy the harm you have done Kristin. I shall ride to Jørundgaard myself tomorrow if you will lend me one of your men and I can get Ingrid north on the hillside to tend my cattle.”
> Lady Aashild came to Jørundgaard the next evening just as the moonlight mingled with the last gleam of daylight. She saw how pale and hollow-cheeked Kristin had become when the young woman came out into the yard to receive the guest.
> The lady sat by the oven and played with the two little ones. Secretly she watched Kristin searchingly as the girl went about setting the table. Thin she was and quiet she looked. She had always been quiet, but another kind of silence lay over the girl now. Lady Aashild guessed all the tension and stubborn defiance that lay behind it.
> “You have surely heard,” said Kristin, coming over to her, “of what has happened here this autumn?”
> “Yes, that my nephew has sued for you?”
> “Do you remember,” asked Kristin, “that you once said he and I might suit each other well? Except that he was far too rich and highborn for me?”
> “I hear Lavrans is of another opinion,” said the lady dryly.
> A gleam flashed in Kristin’s eyes, and she smiled a little. She is good enough, thought Lady Aashild. However little she liked it, she must yield to Erlend and give him the assistance he had asked for.
> Kristin made the guest’s bed in her parents’ bed, and Lady Aashild asked the young woman to sleep with her. After they had lain down and the room had grown quiet, Lady Aashild delivered her message.
> Her heart grew strangely heavy when she saw that this child did not seem to think at all of the sorrow she would cause her parents. I had lived in sorrow and torment with Baard for more than twenty years, thought the lady. But perhaps it is so for us all. Kristin did not even seem to have seen how Ulvhild had declined that autumn—Aashild thought it unlikely she would ever again see the little sister alive. But she said nothing of it—the longer Kristin could sustain this wild joy and exultation, the better it might be.
> Kristin rose, and in the darkness she gathered her jewelry into a small casket, which she brought into bed with her. But then Lady Aashild said:
> “Even so, it seems to me, Kristin, that it would be a better course for Erlend to ride down here when your father returns—to declare openly that he has done you a great wrong—and place his cause in Lavrans’s hands.”
> “Then I believe Father would kill Erlend,” said Kristin.
> “Lavrans will not do that if Erlend refuses to draw sword against his father-in-law,” answered the lady.
> “I do not want Erlend to be humiliated so,” said Kristin. “And I do not want Father to know that Erlend had touched me before he asked for me with honor and good name.”
> “Do you think Lavrans will be less angry,” asked Aashild, “when he hears that you have fled the farm with him, and do you think it will be easier for him to bear? Under the law you will be nothing but Erlend’s concubine as long as you live with him without your father having given you away.”
> “It is another matter,” said Kristin, “if I become Erlend’s concubine when he could not obtain me as lawful wife.”
> Lady Aashild was silent. She thought of how she would have to meet Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn when he came home and learned that his daughter had been stolen away.
> Then Kristin said:
> “I understand, Lady Aashild, that you think me a wicked child. But it has been so here on the farm ever since Father came home from the assembly that every day has been a torment for him as for me. It is best for everyone that this matter now comes to an end.”
> They rode from Jørundgaard in good time the next day and reached Haugen just after noon. Erlend met them in the yard, and Kristin threw herself into his arms without caring about the servant who had accompanied Lady Aashild and her.
> Inside the room she greeted Bjørn Gunnarsøn and then Erlend’s two men as though she had known them well before. Lady Aashild could detect no sign that she was either bashful or afraid. And later, when they sat at table and Erlend laid out his plan, Kristin added her word to the counsel and advised concerning the route: they should ride from Haugen the next evening so late that they would reach Rosten when the moon set, travel in darkness through Sil and past Loptsgaard, then go up along the Otta River to the bridge and on the western side of the Otta and the Laagen by deserted roads as far as the horses could endure. They could rest through the day at one of the spring shielings lying there on the slope, “for as far as Holledis’s assembly district extends, we may meet people who know me.”
> “Have you thought of fodder for the horses?” said Lady Aashild. “You cannot rob people’s spring shielings of fodder in a year like this—if any fodder is to be found there—and you know no one has fodder to sell in this valley this year.”
> “I have thought of it,” answered Kristin. “You must lend us fodder and food for three days. That is also one reason why we ought not travel in so large a company—Erlend must send Jon back to Husaby. The year has been better in Trøndelag, and surely some loads can be carried across the mountain before Christmas. There are some poor people south in the district whom I would like you to help with alms from Erlend and me, Lady Aashild.”
> Bjørn burst into a strange, joyless guffaw. Lady Aashild shook her head. But the servant Ulv lifted his dark, sharp face and looked at Kristin with a peculiar, insolent smile:
> “There is never abundance at Husaby, Kristin Lavransdatter, whether in good years or famine years. But perhaps matters will be different when you take charge there. From your speech, you sound like the mistress Erlend needs.”
> Kristin nodded calmly to the man and continued rapidly. They must keep off the main road as much as possible. And it seemed impossible to her to take the road by Hamar. Erlend objected that Munan was there—it concerned the letter to the duchess.
> “Then Ulv may leave us at Fagaberg and ride to Sir Munan, while we keep west of Mjøsa and ride through Land and by the back roads over Hadeland down to Hakedal. From there, I have heard my uncle speak of a deserted road running south to Margretadal. It is impossible for us to ride across Raumarike at the time when the great wedding is to be held at Dyfrin,” she said, laughing.
> Erlend went over and put his arm around her shoulder, and she leaned back against him without caring about all the people who sat watching it. Lady Aashild said indignantly:
> “No one would think otherwise than that you had run away from home before.”
> And Sir Bjørn guffawed as before.
> A little later Lady Aashild rose to go to the cookhouse and prepare the food. She had kindled the fire there, for Erlend’s men were to sleep there that night. She bade Kristin come with her, “for I wish to be able to swear to Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn that you were not alone together for any time in my house,” she said angrily.
> Kristin laughed and went with the lady. Immediately afterward Erlend came sauntering in to them, pulled a three-legged stool up to the hearth, and sat there obstructing the women. He caught hold of Kristin whenever she came near him as she hurried about her tasks. At last he pulled her down onto his knee.
> “It is true, as Ulv says, that you are the mistress I need.”
> “Oh yes,” said Aashild, laughing and annoyed, “you may be well enough served by her. She is the one who is staking everything in this adventure—you risk little.”
> “That is true,” said Erlend. “But I have shown my willingness to go to her by the proper road. Do not be so angry now, Aunt Aashild.”
> “I must surely be angry,” said the lady. “No sooner had you set your affairs in order than you arranged matters so that you must run away from it all with a woman.”
> “You must remember, Aunt,” said Erlend. “It has always been so that the men who ruined their affairs for a woman’s sake were not the worst of men—all the sagas say so.”
> “Oh, God help us,” said Aashild. Her face became young and soft. “I have heard that speech before, Erlend”—she put her hands around his head and tousled his hair.
> At that moment Ulv Haldorsøn tore open the door and quickly shut it behind him:
> “Another guest has come to the farm, Erlend—the one you would least like to see, I think.”
> “Is it Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn?” said Erlend, springing up.
> “It is not so well,” said the servant. “It is Eline Ormsdatter.”
> The door was opened from outside; the woman who entered pushed Ulv aside and came forward into the light. Kristin looked over at Erlend. At first it was as though he withered and collapsed; then he straightened himself, dark-red in the face.
> “Where the devil have you come from—what do you want here!”
> Lady Aashild stepped forward and said:
> “You must come up into the room, Eline Ormsdatter—we still have enough good custom on this farm that we do not receive our guests in the cookhouse.”
> “I do not expect, Lady Aashild,” said the other, “that Erlend’s kinsmen will greet me as a guest.—You asked where I came from—I come from Husaby, as you may know. I can bring you greetings from Orm and Margret; they are well.”
> Erlend did not answer.
> “When I heard that you had had Gissur Arnfinsøn raise money for you, and that you were going south again,” she continued, “I thought you would likely stop with your kinsmen in Gudbrandsdal this time. I knew you had had inquiries made about the daughter of their neighbor.”
> She looked at Kristin for the first time and met the girl’s eyes. Kristin was very pale, but she looked calmly and searchingly at the other woman.
> Kristin was calm as stone. She had known it from the moment she heard who had come—this was the thought from which she had constantly fled; she had sought to drown it out with defiance and unrest and impatience; all the while she had struggled not to think whether Erlend had wholly and completely freed himself from his former concubine. Now it had overtaken her; it was useless to struggle against it any longer. But she did not plead for herself.
> She saw that Eline Ormsdatter was beautiful. She was no longer young, but she was beautiful, and once she must have been radiantly beautiful. She had pushed back her hood; her head was round as a ball and hard, and her cheekbones protruded somewhat—but one could readily see that she had once been fair despite that. The headcloth covered only the back of her head; while she spoke, Eline smoothed her gold-bright, waving front hair beneath the linen with her hands. Kristin had never seen a woman with such large eyes; they were dark brown, round, and hard, but beneath the narrow coal-black brows and long eyelashes they were wonderfully beautiful against the golden hair. Her skin and lips were chafed from riding in the cold, but it could not disfigure her greatly; she was too beautiful for that. The heavy traveling clothes enveloped her figure, but she bore herself in them as only a woman does who walks in the surest pride in the splendor of her body. She was scarcely as tall as Kristin, but her carriage made her appear taller than the slender, delicately limbed young woman.
> “Has she been with you at Husaby the whole time?” Kristin asked softly.
> “I have not been at Husaby,” said Erlend shortly, flushing again. “I have been at Hestnæs most of this summer.”
> “This is the news I wanted to bring you, Erlend,” said Eline. “You need no longer impose on your kinsmen and test their hospitality because I manage your household. I became a widow this autumn.”
> Erlend stood as before.
> “It was not I who asked you to come to Husaby and manage it last year,” he said with difficulty.
> “I heard that everything lay neglected there,” said Eline. “I retained so much affection for you from former days, Erlend, that I thought I should take care of your welfare—although God knows you have not acted well toward our children or toward me.”
> “For the children I have done what I could,” said Erlend. “And you know well that it was for their sake I endured your remaining at Husaby. You cannot yourself believe that you benefited them or me by it,” he said, smiling mockingly. “Gissur could surely manage there without your help.”
> “Yes, you have always trusted Gissur so greatly,” said Eline, laughing softly. “But this is the matter, Erlend—now I am free. If you wish it, you can now keep the promise you once gave me.”
> Erlend was silent.
> “Do you remember,” asked Eline, “the night I gave birth to your son? Then you promised that you would marry me when Sigurd died.”
> Erlend ran his hand beneath his sweat-wet hair.
> “Yes, I remember it,” he said.
> “Will you keep that word now?” asked Eline.
> “No,” said Erlend.
> Eline Ormsdatter looked over at Kristin—smiled slightly and nodded. Then she looked at Erlend again.
> “It is ten years ago, Eline,” said the man. “Since that time we have lived together year after year like two damned souls in hell.”
> “Not only that, surely!” she said with the same smile.
> “It is years and years since it was anything else,” said Erlend, exhausted. “It cannot help the children. And you know—you know that I can scarcely bear to be in the same room with you any longer,” he almost screamed.
> “I noticed nothing of that when you were home this summer,” said Eline with a meaningful smile. “Then we were not enemies—always.”
> “If you count it that we were friends, then gladly, for all I care,” said Erlend wearily.
> “Will you stand here?” said Lady Aashild. She ladled from the pot into two large wooden dishes and gave one to Kristin. The girl received it. “Carry it over—and you, Ulv, take the other—set them on the table; supper we must have whether matters stand this way or that.”
> Kristin and the servant went with the dishes of food. Lady Aashild said to the other two:
> “Come now, you too; it is useless for you to stand here barking at each other.”
> “It is better that Eline and I speak matters out now,” said Erlend.
> Lady Aashild made no further answer, but left.
> In the room Kristin had set the food on the table and fetched ale from the cellar. She sat on the outer bench, straight as a candle and calm in the face, but she did not eat. Neither Bjørn nor Erlend’s men had much appetite either. Only the man who had come with Eline and Bjørn’s farmhand ate. Lady Aashild sat down and ate a little porridge. No one uttered a word.
> At long last Eline Ormsdatter entered alone. Lady Aashild offered her a place between Kristin and herself; Eline sat and ate something. From time to time a reflection of a hidden smile flickered over her face, and she glanced sidelong at Kristin.
> After a while Lady Aashild went out to the cookhouse.
> The fire on the hearth had nearly burned out. Erlend sat there on the three-legged stool, hunched up with his head down in his arms.
> Lady Aashild went over and laid her hand on his shoulder:
> “God forgive you, Erlend, for how you have arranged this—!”
> Erlend looked up; his face was smeared with misery.
> “She is with child,” he said, closing his eyes.
> Lady Aashild’s face flamed; she gripped his shoulder hard:
> “Which of them?” she asked coarsely and mockingly.
> “Well, it is not mine,” said Erlend, as lifeless as before. “But you will not believe me—no one will—” He collapsed together again.
> Lady Aashild sat before him on the edge of the hearth.
> “You must try to take courage now, Erlend. It is not easy to believe you in this matter. Do you swear it is not yours?”
> Erlend lifted his ravaged face:
> “As surely as I need God’s mercy—as surely as I hope that—that God has comforted Mother with him for all she had to endure down here—I have not touched Eline since the first time I saw Kristin!” He shouted it, so that Lady Aashild hushed him.
> “Then I do not understand that this is such a misfortune. You must find out who the father is and buy him into marrying her.”
> “I think it is Gissur Arnfinsøn—my steward at Husaby,” said Erlend wearily. “We spoke of it last autumn—and since then too—. Sigurd’s death has been expected for a while. He was willing to marry her when she became a widow if I would give her a suitable dowry—”
> “Yes,” said Lady Aashild. Erlend continued:
> “She swears by damnation that she will not have him. She will name me as the father. If I swear myself free—do you think anyone will believe anything but that I swear falsely—”
> “You must be able to turn her from this,” said Lady Aashild. “There is now no other course: tomorrow itself you must go home with her to Husaby. And then you must be hard and firm and have this marriage arranged between your steward and Eline.”
> “Yes,” said Erlend. Then he threw himself forward and sobbed aloud.
> “Do you not understand, Aunt—what do you think Kristin will believe—”
