> During the night Erlend lay in the cookhouse with the servants. In the room Kristin slept with Lady Aashild in her bed, and Eline Ormsdatter in the other bed there. Bjørn went out and lay down in the stable.
> The next morning Kristin accompanied Lady Aashild to the cowshed. While the lady went into the cookhouse to prepare the morning meal, Kristin carried the milk up into the room.
> A candle burned on the table. Eline was dressed and sat on the edge of her bed. Kristin greeted her quietly, fetched a strainer, and strained the milk.
> “Will you give me a drink of milk?” asked Eline. Kristin took a wooden ladle and handed it to the other woman; she drank greedily and looked over the rim at Kristin.
> “So you are that Kristin Lavransdatter who has parted me from Erlend’s friendship,” she said, returning the ladle.
> “You must know yourself whether there was any friendship to part,” answered the young woman.
> Eline bit her lip.
> “What will you do,” she said, “if Erlend grows weary of you and one day offers to marry you to his servant? Will you obey Erlend in that too?”
> Kristin did not answer; then the other laughed and said:
> “Now you obey him in all things, I imagine. What do you think, Kristin—shall we cast dice for our man, we two concubines of Erlend Nikulaussøn?” When she received no answer, she laughed again and said: “Are you so simple that you do not deny you are a concubine?”
> “For you I will not trouble to lie,” said Kristin.
> “It would not greatly profit you either,” answered Eline as before. “I know the lad. He likely displayed himself before you like a cock grouse the second time you were together, I suppose. It is a pity for you too, fair child standing there.”
> Kristin’s cheeks whitened. Sick with disgust, she said softly:
> “I will not speak with you—”
> “Do you think he will treat you better than me?” Eline began. Then Kristin answered sharply:
> “I shall not complain of Erlend, whatever he does. I myself went out upon the wrong path—and I shall not whine or pity myself if it leads over the precipice—”
> Eline was silent for a while. Then she said, red and uncertain:
> “I too was a maiden when he took me, Kristin—though I had been called that old man’s wife for seven years. But you cannot understand what a wretched life it was.”
> Kristin began to tremble violently. Eline looked at her. Then she took from her traveling chest, which stood beside her on the bed-step, a little horn. She broke the seal and said softly:
> “You are young and I am old, Kristin. I know well that it is useless for me to contend against you—now is your time. Will you drink with me, Kristin?”
> Kristin did not move. Then the other raised the horn to her mouth. Kristin noticed that she did not drink. Eline said:
> “You can surely show me the honor of drinking to me—and promise that you will not be a harsh stepmother to my children?”
> Kristin took the horn. At that moment Erlend opened the door. He stood for a while and looked from one woman to the other.
> “What is this?” he asked.
> Then Kristin answered, her voice shrill and wild:
> “We are drinking to each other, we two concubines of yours—”
> He seized her wrist and took the horn. “Be silent,” he said sternly. “You shall not drink with her.”
> “Why not?” said Kristin as before. “She was as pure as I when you enticed her—”
> “She has said that so often that I believe she believes it herself,” said Erlend. “Do you remember when you made me go to Sigurd with that tale, and he produced witnesses that he had caught you before with another man?”
> White with revulsion, Kristin turned away. Eline had become dark red; then she said defiantly:
> “She will not become a leper merely by drinking with me.”
> Erlend turned angrily toward Eline—then suddenly his face seemed to grow long and rigid, and the man gasped with terror:
> “Jesus!” he said almost inaudibly. He seized Eline by the arm.
> “Drink to her, then,” he said harshly and trembling. “You drink first, and then she shall drink with you.”
> Eline twisted away with a groan. She fled backward into the room; the man followed. “Drink,” he said. He tore the dagger from his belt and followed with it in his hand. “Drink the draught you poured for Kristin.” He seized Eline by the arm and dragged her to the table, forcing her forward toward the horn.
> Eline screamed once and hid her face against her arm. Erlend released her and stood trembling.
> “I had a hell with Sigurd,” screamed Eline. “You—you promised—but you have been worse toward me, Erlend!”
> Then Kristin stepped forward and seized the horn:
> “One of us two must drink—you cannot keep us both—”
> Erlend took the horn from her and flung her across the floor, so that she fell near Lady Aashild’s bed. He thrust the drink toward Eline Ormsdatter’s mouth—stood with one knee on the bench beside her and, with his hand around her head, tried to force the drink into her.
> She reached beneath his arm, snatched the dagger from the table, and struck at the man. The thrust scarcely did more than graze his clothes. Then she turned the point against herself, and immediately afterward sank sideways into his lap.
> Kristin rose and came over. Erlend held Eline; her head hung backward over his arm. She began to rattle almost at once—there was blood in her throat, and it ran from her mouth. She spat out a great quantity and said:
> “For you I had intended—that draught—for all the times—you have betrayed me—”
> “Bring Aunt Aashild here,” said Erlend quietly. Kristin stood motionless.
> “She is dying,” said Erlend as before.
> “Then she fares better than we,” said Kristin. Erlend looked at her—the despair in his eyes softened her. She went out of the room.
> “What is it?” asked Lady Aashild when Kristin called her out of the cookhouse.
> “We have killed Eline Ormsdatter,” said Kristin. “She is dying—”
> Lady Aashild broke into a run. But Eline breathed her last as the lady stepped through the door.
> Lady Aashild had laid the dead woman straight on the bench, washed the blood from her face, and covered it with the head linen. Erlend stood leaning against the wall behind the corpse.
> “Do you understand,” said Lady Aashild, “that this was the worst thing that could happen?”
> She had filled the oven with wood and twigs; now she placed the horn in the midst of it and blew the fire into flame.
> “Can you trust your servants?” the lady asked again. “Ulv and Haftor, I mean. Jon and the man who came with Eline I know little.”
> “You understand,” said the lady, “if it becomes known that you and Kristin were together here and that you were alone with her when she died, then you might just as well have let Kristin drink Eline’s brew. And if there is talk of poison, people will remember what I have been accused of before. Did she have any kinsmen or friends?”
> “No,” said Erlend quietly. “She had no one except me.”
> “Even so,” Lady Aashild continued, “it may be difficult to conceal this and dispose of the body without the foulest suspicion falling upon you.”
> “She shall lie in consecrated earth,” said Erlend, “even if it costs me Husaby. What do you say, Kristin?”
> Kristin nodded.
> Lady Aashild sat silent. The more she thought, the more impossible it seemed to find a way out. Four servants sat in the cookhouse—even if Erlend could buy them all into silence, even if one of them, Eline’s servant, could be bought into leaving the country, it would still remain uncertain. And at Jørundgaard they knew Kristin had been here—if Lavrans learned this, she could not imagine what he would do. Then there was the disposal of the dead woman. The mountain road westward was unthinkable now—the choices were the road to Raumsdal, across the mountain to Trondheim, or south through the valley. And if the truth emerged, it would never be believed—even if it were accepted as true.
> “I must take counsel with Bjørn about this,” she said, rising and going out.
> Bjørn Gunnarsøn listened to his wife’s account without moving a muscle and without taking his eyes from Erlend.
> “Bjørn,” said Aashild desperately. “Someone must swear that he saw her lay hands upon herself.”
> Life slowly darkened in Bjørn’s eyes; he looked at his wife, and his mouth drew into a crooked smile:
> “You mean that this someone should be I?”
> Lady Aashild wrung her hands and raised them toward him. “Bjørn, you understand what is at stake for these two—”
> “And then you think that with me all is over anyway?” he asked slowly. “Or do you think enough remains of the man I once was that I dare swear a false oath to save that boy there from ruin?
> “I who myself was dragged under—all those years ago. Dragged under, I said,” he insisted.
> “You say that because I am old now,” whispered Aashild.
> Kristin burst into tears, so that the sound cut through the room. She had sat in the corner by Aashild’s bed, rigid and silent. Now she began to weep aloud. It was as though Lady Aashild’s voice had torn her heart open. It had been heavy with memories of the sweetness of love; it was as though that voice first made Kristin fully comprehend what her and Erlend’s love had been. The memory of hot and ardent happiness flooded over everything else—washed away the hard, despairing hatred from the last night. She knew only her love and her will to endure.
> All three looked at her. Then Sir Bjørn went over, took her under the chin, and looked down at her:
> “Do you say, Kristin, that she did it herself?”
> “Every word you have heard is true,” said Kristin firmly. “We threatened her until she did this.”
> “She had intended a worse fate for Kristin,” said Aashild.
> Sir Bjørn released the girl. He went to the corpse, lifted it into the bed where Eline had lain the night before, laid it close to the wall, and pulled the blankets well over it.
> “You must send Jon and the servant you do not know home to Husaby with the message that Eline is accompanying you southward. Let them ride at noon. Say the women are sleeping in here; they can eat in the cookhouse. Then you must speak with Ulv and Haftor. Has she threatened to do this before? So that you can produce testimony to it if such things are asked?”
> “Every person who was on the farm during the last years we lived there together,” said Erlend wearily, “can testify that she threatened to kill herself—and me too, sometimes—when I spoke of separating from her.”
> Bjørn laughed harshly:
> “I thought so. Tonight we must dress her in her traveling clothes and set her in the sledge. You must sit beside her—”
> Erlend swayed where he stood:
> “I cannot.”
> “God knows how much man will remain in you after you have governed yourself for another twenty years,” said Bjørn. “Do you think you can drive the sledge, then? I shall sit beside her. We must travel by night and by deserted roads until we are down in Fron. In this cold no one can know how long she has been dead. We shall drive into the monks’ hospice at Roaldstad. There you and I shall testify that you quarreled in the back of the sledge. It is well attested that you have not wished to live with her since you were released from the ban, and that you have sued for a maiden who is your equal. Ulv and Haftor must keep away from us the entire journey so that they can swear, if need be, that she was alive the last time they saw her. You can surely get them to do that? With the monks you can have her placed in a coffin—and then you must bargain with the priests for peace in the grave for her and peace of soul for yourself.
> “Yes, it is not pretty. But you have not arranged matters so that they can be prettier. Do not stand there like a woman in childbed about to faint. God help you, boy, you have evidently never before felt the edge against the knot at your throat.”
> A biting wind came down from the mountain—fine, silver-bright snow smoke rose from the drifts into the moon-blue air when the men were to drive away.
> Two horses had been harnessed one before the other. Erlend sat in front of the sledge. Kristin went over to him:
> “This time, Erlend, you must try to send me word of how the journey goes and where you end up.”
> He squeezed her hand so hard she thought the blood must burst from the roots of her nails.
> “Do you still dare hold fast to me, Kristin?”
> “Yes, still,” she said, and a little later: “In this deed we are both guilty—I incited you, for I wanted her death.”
> Lady Aashild and Kristin stood watching the party. The sledge dipped up and down over the drifts. It disappeared into a hollow—emerged farther down on a white field. But then the men passed into the shadow of a ridge and vanished for good.
> The two women sat before the oven with their backs to the empty bed from which Lady Aashild had carried out the clothes and straw. Both were aware that it stood empty and gaping behind them.
> “Would you like us to sleep in the cookhouse tonight?” said Lady Aashild once.
> “It will surely be the same wherever we lie,” said Kristin.
> Lady Aashild went out and looked at the weather.
> “Yes, if the wind rises or a thaw comes, they will not get far before it is discovered,” said Kristin.
> “It always blows here at Haugen,” answered Lady Aashild. “There is no sign of a change in the weather.”
> Then they sat as before.
> “You must not forget,” said the lady once, “what fate she had intended for you.”
> Kristin answered softly:
> “I think that perhaps I would have wished the same in her place.”
> “You would never have wished to cause another human being to become leprous,” said Lady Aashild vehemently.
> “Do you remember, Aunt, that you once said to me: it is good when one does not dare do what one does not think fair. But it is not so good when one thinks a thing is not fair because one does not dare do it.”
> “You would not dare do it because of the sin,” said Lady Aashild.
> “No, I do not think so,” said Kristin. “It is true that I have already done much that I once would have believed I dared not do because of the sin. But I did not see then that sin carries this with it: one must trample others down.”
> “Erlend wished to end his dissolute life long before he met you,” answered Aashild vehemently. “It was over between the two of them before.”
> “I know,” said Kristin. “But she had surely never been given reason to believe that Erlend’s resolves were so firm that she could not shake them.”
> “Kristin,” Lady Aashild begged anxiously, “you will not abandon Erlend now, surely. Now you cannot be saved unless you save each other.”
> “A priest would scarcely advise thus,” said Kristin, smiling coldly. “But I know that I shall not abandon Erlend—not even if I must trample down my own father.”
> Lady Aashild rose.
> “We might as well find something to do instead of sitting like this,” she said. “It would be vain to try to go to rest.”
> She fetched the butter churn from the chamber, carried in several tubs of milk, poured it in, and positioned herself to churn.
> “Let me do this,” Kristin begged. “I have the younger back.”
> They worked without speaking; Kristin stood by the chamber door churning, and Aashild carded wool near the hearth. Only when Kristin had strained the buttermilk from the churn and was kneading the butter did the young woman suddenly ask:
> “Aunt Aashild—are you never afraid of the day when you shall stand before God’s judgment?”
> Lady Aashild rose, came over, and stood before Kristin in the light:
> “Perhaps I shall have courage to ask him who created me as I am whether he will have mercy on me when his time comes. For I have never asked for his mercy when I acted against his commandment. And never have I asked God or man to remit one penny of the penance I have paid here in this world.”
> A little later she said softly:
> “Munan, my eldest son, was twenty years old. He was not then as I know he is now. They were not like that then, my children—”
> Kristin answered quietly:
> “Yet you have had Sir Bjørn at your side every day and every night throughout all these years.”
> “Yes—that too I have had,” said Aashild.
> A little later Kristin had finished making the butter. Lady Aashild then said they should try to lie down for a while.
> Inside the dark bed she put her arm around Kristin’s shoulder and drew the young head close to her. It was not long before she heard from Kristin’s even, quiet breathing that she had fallen asleep.
> 
> IV.
> 
> The frost held. In every cowshed in the district the half-starved animals lowed and complained and suffered from the cold. But already the people were saving fodder to the utmost of their ability.
> There was little visiting at Christmas that year; people stayed home, each at his own place.
> At Christmas the cold increased—it was as though every day seemed colder than the one before. People could scarcely remember so severe a winter. No more snow fell, not even in the mountains, but the snow that had fallen around Saint Clement’s Day froze hard as stone. The sun shone from a clear sky now that the days began to brighten. At night the northern lights crackled and flickered over the mountain ridges in the north; they flickered over half the sky—but brought no change in the weather. Now and then a day would cloud over, a little dry snow would sift down, and then clear weather and bitter cold returned. The Laagen murmured and clucked dully beneath its bridges of ice.
> Every morning Kristin thought that now she could bear no more; she would not endure to the end of that day. For she felt each day to be like single combat between her father and herself. And must they stand opposed like this now, when every living creature, man and beast, throughout the districts suffered under a trial? But when evening came, she had endured.
> It was not that her father was unfriendly. They never spoke of what lay between them, but behind everything he did not say she felt that he was inflexibly resolved to persist in his refusal.
> And longing for his friendship burned within her. It burned so terribly because she knew how much else her father had to bear—and had it been as before, he would have spoken with her about it. It was true that they were better provided for at Jørundgaard than at most other places, but here too they felt the famine every day and every hour. In other winters Lavrans had been accustomed to break and train his young horses, but that autumn he had sold them all in the south of the country. His daughter missed hearing his voice in the yard and seeing him struggle with the slender, shaggy two-year-olds in the sport he loved so much. The barns, storehouses, and bins had not been full after the preceding harvest, and many people also came to Jørundgaard asking for help, whether to buy or as a gift, and no one asked in vain.
> Late one evening a huge old man clad in skins came on skis. Lavrans spoke with him in the yard, and Halvdan carried food to him in the hearth-room. No one on the farm who had seen him knew who he was—presumably he was one of the people who lived in the mountains; perhaps Lavrans had encountered him there. But her father did not mention the visit, nor did Halvdan.
> But one evening a man came with whom Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn had had a dispute for many years. Lavrans went with him to the storehouse. But when he came back into the room, he said:
> “They all want help from me. But here on my farm you are all against me. You too, wife,” he said vehemently to Ragnfrid.
> Then the mother sprang toward Kristin.
> “Do you hear what your father says to me! I am not against you, Lavrans. You know it too, Kristin, what happened down here at Roaldstad late in the autumn, when he traveled down the valley in company with that other adulterer, his kinsman from Haugen—the wretched woman whose own life she took, whom he had enticed away from all her kinsmen.”
> Rigid and hard, Kristin answered:
> “I understand that you blame him as much for the years he has labored to escape from sin as for the years he lived in it.”
> “Jesus, Mary,” cried Ragnfrid, striking her hands together. “What have you become! Could not even this cause you to change your mind!”
> “No,” said Kristin. “I have not changed my mind.”
> Then Lavrans looked up from the bench where he sat with Ulvhild:
> “Nor have I, Kristin,” he said quietly.
> But Kristin knew in her heart that in a manner she had changed—if not her mind, then her view. She had received word of how that journey of misfortune had ended. It had gone more easily than one might expect. Whether cold had settled in the wound or whatever the cause, the knife-thrust Erlend had received in the breast had gone awry; he had lain ill for a time at the hospice at Roaldstad. Sir Bjørn had tended him during those days. But because Erlend was wounded it had been easier to explain the other matter and be believed.
> When he could travel onward, he had carried the dead woman with him in a coffin all the way to Oslo. There, through Sira Jon’s intercession, he had evidently obtained a burial place for her in the graveyard of the ruined Church of Saint Nicholas; then he had confessed to the Bishop of Oslo himself, and the bishop had laid upon him a pilgrimage to the Holy Blood at Schwerin. Now he had departed the country.
> She could not make pilgrimage to any place and find absolution. For her there was only to sit here, wait, think, and try to endure in her resistance to her parents. A strange, wintry light fell over all the memories of her meetings with Erlend. She thought of his violence—in love and in sorrow—and it seemed to her that if she too had been able to take up every matter just as suddenly and storm with it at once, then afterward it might have seemed smaller and easier to bear. It happened that she thought: perhaps Erlend will give me up. She thought she had always had a little fear that if matters became too difficult for them, he would give up. But she would not give him up unless he himself released her from every oath.
> So the winter wore on. And Kristin could no longer deceive herself; she had to see that the hardest trial of all now awaited them, for Ulvhild did not have long to live. And amid her bitter sorrow for her sister she saw with horror that her own soul truly had been led astray and consumed by sin. For with the dying child and her parents’ inexpressible sorrow before her eyes, she still thought of this: if Ulvhild dies, how shall I endure looking at Father and not cast myself down before him, confess everything, and beg him to forgive me and govern me—.
> They were in Lent. People slaughtered what remained of the small herds they had hoped to keep alive, lest the cattle die by themselves. And the people grew sick from living on fish and having so little miserable flour food. Sira Eirik released the whole district from the prohibition against milk foods. But the people scarcely had a drop of milk.
> Ulvhild lay abed. She lay alone in the sisters’ bed, and someone kept watch over her every night. It happened that her father and Kristin both sat with her. On such a night Lavrans said to his daughter:
> “Do you remember what Brother Edvin said about Ulvhild’s fate? I thought even then that perhaps he meant this. But I thrust it from me.”
> During those nights he sometimes spoke of one thing and another from the time when the children were small. Kristin sat white and desperate and understood that behind his words her father was pleading with her.
> One day Lavrans went out with Kolbein to seek a bear’s den in the mountain forest to the north. They came home with a she-bear on a sledge, and Lavrans had a living bear cub inside the breast of his tunic. It amused Ulvhild a little when he showed it to her. But Ragnfrid said this was hardly a time to take on such an animal, and what did he intend to do with it now?
> “I shall rear it to tether before my maidens’ storehouse,” said Lavrans, laughing harshly.
> But they could not provide the cub with the rich milk it needed, and so Lavrans killed it a few days later.
> The sun gained enough power that sometimes, at midday, water dripped a little from the roofs. The tits clung and climbed on the sunny sides of the timber walls, pecking so that it rang as they sought flies sleeping in the cracks between the logs. Across the fields the snow shone hard and bright as silver.
> At last, one evening, clouds began to draw across the moon. In the morning they awoke at Jørundgaard in a whirl of snow through which they could see nothing in any direction.
> That day they understood that Ulvhild would die.
> The entire household was inside, and Sira Eirik came over. Many candles burned in the room. Early in the evening Ulvhild quietly and peacefully expired in her mother’s arms.
> Ragnfrid bore it better than anyone had expected. The parents sat beside each other, both weeping very quietly. Everyone in the room wept. When Kristin went over to her father, he put his arm around her shoulder. He felt how she shook and trembled, and then he drew her close. But it seemed to her that he must feel as though she were farther removed from him than the little dead child in the bed.
> She did not understand how she endured. She scarcely remembered herself what she endured for, but numb and mute with pain, she remained standing and did not fall at his feet.
> Then a pair of planks were broken up in the church floor before Saint Thomas’s altar, and in the stone-hard earth beneath, a grave was hewn for Ulvhild Lavransdatter.
> It snowed thickly and quietly all the days the child lay upon the bier; it snowed when she was carried to the earth, and it continued snowing, almost without pause, for a whole month afterward.
> To the people who went about waiting for spring’s release, it seemed that it would never come. The days grew long and light, and the valley lay in a vapor of melting snow while the sun shone. But the cold remained in the air, and the warmth gained no power. At night it froze hard—the ice cracked, deep booms came from within the mountains, and wolves howled and foxes barked all the way down in the district as at midwinter. People stripped bark for the cattle, but the animals fell dead in great numbers in their stalls. No one understood how this would end.
> Kristin went out on such a day, when water trickled in the ruts and the snow glittered like silver over the fields. Facing the sun, the drifts were already hollowed out, so that the fine lattice of ice along the crust’s edge broke with a soft silver sound when she touched it with her foot. But everywhere, in the smallest shadow, the keen cold remained in the air and the snow was hard.
> She went up toward the church—she did not herself know what she wanted there, but she was drawn thither. Her father was there—some farmers, guild brothers, had a meeting in the porch, she knew.
> Up on the hill she met the group of farmers coming down. Sira Eirik was with them. All the men were on foot; they went in a dark, shaggy cluster, hunched over and not speaking together; they returned her greeting curtly as she passed.
> Kristin thought it was long since the time when every person in the district had been her friend. Now they all surely knew she was a wicked daughter. Perhaps they knew more about her too. Now they probably all believed there had been some truth in the old talk about her and Arne and Bentein. Perhaps she was in the worst repute. She lifted her head and continued to the church.
> The door stood ajar. It was cold inside the church, yet a kind of warmth flowed toward her mind from this dim, brown space, with the soaring trunks of pillars that lifted the darkness toward the roof’s framework. No lights were burning on the altars, but a little sunlight entered through the crack of the door and glimmered faintly on images and vessels.
> Before Saint Thomas’s altar she saw her father kneeling, his head bowed toward his folded hands, which pressed his cap against his breast.
> Shy and sorrowful, Kristin slipped out and stood in the porch. Framed by the arch between the two small pillars she held, she saw Jørundgaard lying below, and behind her home the pale-blue haze over the valley. In the sun the river flashed white with water and ice through the district. But the alder scrub along its course was golden-brown with catkins; the spruce forest was spring-green nevertheless up here by the church, and small birds chirped, twittered, and whistled in the grove nearby. Ah yes, there had been such birdsong every evening after sunset.
> And she felt that the longing she had thought must have been tormented out of her—the longing in her blood and body—now stirred, frail and faint, as though awakening from winter sleep.
> Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn came out and locked the church door behind him. He went over and stood near his daughter, looking out through the next arch. She saw how this winter had ravaged her father. She did not understand how she could touch upon this now, but it burst from her all the same.
> “Is it true, as Mother said the other day, that you told her—had it been Arne Gyrdsøn, you would have yielded to me?”
> “Yes,” said Lavrans without looking at her.
> “You did not say so while Arne lived,” answered Kristin.
> “It never arose. I understood well enough that the boy loved you—but he said nothing—and he was young—and I never noticed that you thought of him in that way. You could hardly expect me to offer my daughter to a man who owned nothing.” He smiled fleetingly. “But I was fond of the boy,” he said softly. “And had I seen you tormented by love for him—”
> They remained standing and looking out. Kristin felt her father looking at her—she struggled to keep her face calm, but she felt herself turn white. Then her father went to her, put both arms around her, and crushed her in his embrace. He bent her head backward, looked down into his daughter’s face, then hid it again against his shoulder.
> “Jesus Christ, little Kristin, are you then so unhappy—”
> “I think I shall die of it, Father,” she said against him.
> She burst into tears. But she wept because she had felt in his caress and seen in his eyes that now he was so tormented he could no longer hold fast to his resistance. She had prevailed over him.
> In the night she awoke when her father touched her shoulder in the darkness:
> “Get up,” he said softly. “Do you hear—?”
> Then she heard the sound singing around the corners of the house—the deep, full note of a moisture-laden south wind. Water streamed from the roof; rain whispered as it fell into soft, thawing snow.
> Kristin threw on her gown and followed her father to the outer door. Together they stood looking into the bright May night; warm wind and rain struck them—the sky was a turmoil of tangled, driving rain clouds; the forests roared; the wind whistled between the houses—and from the mountain they heard the dull thunder of snow sliding outward.
> Kristin sought her father’s hand and held it. He had called her and wanted to show her this. It had been so between them before that he would have done that. And now it was so again.
> When they went inside again to lie down, Lavrans said:
> “The foreign servant who was here this week carried a letter to me from Sir Munan Baardsøn. He intends to come up here this summer to visit his mother, and he asked whether he might find me and speak with me then.”
> “What will you answer him, my father?” she whispered.
> “I cannot tell you now,” answered Lavrans. “But I shall speak with him, and then I must govern the matter as I believe I can answer for it before God, my daughter.”
> Kristin crept back beside Ramborg, and Lavrans went and lay down beside his sleeping wife. He lay thinking that if the flood came great and sudden, few farms in the district were as exposed as Jørundgaard. There was said to be a prophecy that one day the river would take it.
> 
> V.
> 
> Spring came in a single rush. A few days after the thaw broke, the district lay black-brown beneath the downpours. Water poured down the slopes; the river rose and lay like a lead-gray sea in the valley bottom, with small floating groves at the surface and a treacherously bubbling furrow of current. At Jørundgaard the water stood far in over the fields. Yet everywhere the damage was less than people had feared.
> The spring sowing came late, and the people sowed their scant seed grain with a prayer to God that he might spare it from night frost until autumn. And it appeared that now he would hear them and lighten their burdens somewhat. June came with changeable weather, the summer was good, and the people turned toward the hope that the traces of famine would in time be erased.
> The haymaking was over when, one evening, four mounted men came to Jørundgaard. They were two lords with two servants: Sir Munan Baardsøn and Sir Baard Petersøn of Hestnæs.
> Ragnfrid and Lavrans had the table laid in the upper loft-room and beds prepared for the guests in the storehouse. But Lavrans asked the lords to wait until the next day, when they had rested after their journey, before presenting their errand.
> Sir Munan led the conversation during the meal; he frequently addressed Kristin and spoke as though they were well acquainted. She saw that her father did not like this.
> Sir Munan was short and stocky, red-faced, ugly, talkative, and somewhat foolish in manner. People called him Munan Stump or Munan Dance. Yet beneath this manner Lady Aashild’s son was a sensible and capable man, who had acted as the Crown’s agent in several matters and evidently had influence with those who governed the realm. He occupied his mother’s hereditary estate in Skogheimsherred, was very rich, and had made a rich marriage. Lady Katrin, his wife, was remarkably ugly and seldom opened her mouth, but her husband always spoke of her as though she were the wisest of women, so in jest people called her Lady Katrin the Wise in Counsel and Fair in Speech. They seemed to live well and lovingly together, although Sir Munan was notorious for his wanton conduct both before and after his marriage.
> Sir Baard Petersøn was a handsome and imposing old man, though by now somewhat heavy-bellied and ponderous of limb. His hair and beard had faded somewhat, but still held as much yellow as white. Since King Magnus Haakonsøn’s death he had lived quietly and managed his great properties in Nordmøre. He was a widower after his second wife and had many children, all of whom were said to be handsome, well reared, and prosperous.
> The next day Lavrans and his guests went to confer in the loft. Lavrans asked his wife to be present, but she would not. “This must lie wholly in your hands. You know it will be the heaviest sorrow for our daughter if this matter does not prosper, but I see well enough that there is not a little that may be said against this marriage.”
> Sir Munan produced a letter from Erlend Nikulaussøn. Erlend offered that Lavrans might himself determine every condition if he would betroth his daughter Kristin to him. Erlend was willing to have his properties valued and his income examined by impartial men, and to give Kristin such a bridal gift and morning gift that she would own one-third of his estate apart from her own dowry and any inheritance she might receive from her kinsmen, should she become his widow without children who survived their father. Furthermore, he offered to let Kristin govern with full authority her share of the estate, both what she owned from home and what she received from him. But if Lavrans wished other conditions for the household settlement, Erlend would gladly hear him and comply. The sole condition to which Kristin’s kinsmen must bind themselves in return was that, if they became guardians of children born to him and her, they would never attempt to overturn the gifts he had made to his children by Eline Ormsdatter, but would allow it to stand that this property had passed out of his estate before he entered marriage with Kristin Lavransdatter. Finally, Erlend offered to hold the wedding with all seemly splendor at his own estate of Husaby.
> Lavrans began to speak and said:
> “This is a fair offer. I understand that it lies greatly upon your kinsman’s heart to reach agreement with me. I see it also from the fact that he has prevailed upon you, Sir Munan, to travel for a second time on such an errand to a man like me, who is of little account beyond this district, and that a lord such as you, Sir Baard, has troubled yourself with this journey to lend support. But now I will say this of Erlend’s offer: my daughter was not reared to govern goods and wealth herself; I have always intended to give her to a man of such a kind that I could safely place the maiden’s welfare in his hands. I do not know whether Kristin is fitted to be set in such authority, but I scarcely think she will thrive with it. She is gentle and yielding in disposition—and this is one of the reasons I have borne in mind in opposing this marriage: Erlend has shown want of judgment in several matters. Had she been a woman eager to rule, bold and contentious, then the matter would at once have stood differently.”
> Sir Munan burst out laughing and said:
> “Dear Lavrans, do you complain that the maiden is not contentious enough—” and Sir Baard said with a slight smile:
> “Yet your daughter seems to me to have shown that she does not lack will—for two years now she has held fast to Erlend directly against you.”
> Lavrans said:
> “I remember it well enough; nevertheless I know what I am saying. It has been painful for her herself during this time when she has defied me, and she will not long be happy with a husband unless he can govern her.”
> “The devil take that,” said Sir Munan. “Then your daughter must be very unlike every woman I have known, for I have never seen one who did not prefer to govern both herself and her husband as well.”
> Lavrans shrugged and did not answer.
> Baard Petersøn then said:
> “I can imagine, Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn, that you have grown no more inclined toward this marriage between your daughter and my foster son since matters ended as they did with the woman he had with him. But you must know that it has come to light that this poor woman had allowed herself to be enticed by another man, Erlend’s steward at Husaby. Erlend knew this when he traveled with her down through the valley; he had offered to provide her a seemly dowry if the man would marry her.”
> “Are you certain this is so?” asked Lavrans. “Even so, I do not know that the matter becomes fairer thereby. It must be hard for a woman of good lineage to enter the farm on the master’s arm but be led out with the servant.”
> Munan Baardsøn broke in:
> “I understand this, Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn: the chief thing you hold against my cousin is that he had this misfortune with Sigurd Saksulvsøn’s wife. And it is true that it was not well. But you must remember, man, in God’s name—there the young lad went about the house with a young and beautiful wife, and she had an old, cold, impotent husband—and the night is half a year long up there; I do not think one could have expected much else unless Erlend had been a wholly holy man. It cannot be denied that Erlend has never had a monk’s flesh, but I do not think your young, beautiful daughter would thank you either if you gave her to a monkish man. It is true that Erlend afterward managed matters foolishly and worse. But this affair must end sometime—we his kinsmen have labored to help the boy to his feet again, the woman is dead, and Erlend has done all he could for her body and soul; the Bishop of Oslo himself has absolved him from his sin, and now he has returned home purified by the Holy Blood at Schwerin—will you be stricter than the Bishop of Oslo and the archbishop there, or whoever governs that precious blood?
> “Dear Lavrans, chastity is a fair thing, but truly no grown man can maintain it without a special gift of grace from God. By Saint Olav—you must remember that the holy king himself did not receive that gift until near the end of his life here below—presumably it was God’s will that he should first beget the worthy boy King Magnus, who broke the heathens’ assault upon the northern lands. King Olav did not have that son with his queen, and yet he sits among the highest saints in Heaven. Yes, I see by your face that you find this unseemly speech—”
> Sir Baard interrupted:
> “Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn, I liked this matter no better than you when Erlend first came to me and said he had set his heart upon a maiden who was promised to another. But since then I have understood that there is so strong a love between these two young people that it would be a great sin to sever their friendship. Erlend was with me at the last Christmas feast King Haakon held for his men; there they met, and as soon as they saw each other, your daughter swooned and lay as dead for a long while—but I saw from my foster son that he would rather lose his life than her.”
> Lavrans sat a while before answering:
> “Yes, such things seem fair when one hears them in a romance of chivalry from the southern lands. But we are not in Britain, and you too would surely require more of a man you were to take as son-in-law than that he had caused your daughter to faint from love before all people’s eyes—”
> The other two were silent; then Lavrans continued:
> “I think this, good lords: had Erlend Nikulaussøn not greatly diminished both his estate and his reputation, you would not sit here and so earnestly beg a man in my circumstances to give him my daughter. But I do not wish it said of Kristin that she was honored when she married into Husaby with a man of one of the country’s noblest lineages—after the man had disgraced himself so that he could expect no better marriage or uphold his lineage’s standing.”
> He rose abruptly, went out onto the floor, and paced up and down.
> But Sir Munan sprang up:
> “No, Lavrans, if you speak of disgrace, then by God you must know you are far too proud—”
> Sir Baard interrupted him; he went over to Lavrans:
> “Proud you are, Lavrans—you are like those farmers we have heard of in former times who would not accept titles from kings because their pride could not endure that people should say they owed thanks to anyone but themselves. I tell you, had Erlend possessed all the honor and wealth to which the lad was born, I still would not count myself diminished when I asked a well-born and prosperous man to give his daughter to my foster son, if I understood that the two young people’s hearts would break should they be separated. Especially,” he said softly, laying a hand on the other man’s shoulder, “if matters stood so that it was best for the salvation of both their souls that they should marry each other.”
> Lavrans drew away from the other’s hand; his face became closed and cold:
> “I surely do not understand what you mean, lord.”
> The two men looked at each other for a while; then Sir Baard said:
> “I mean that Erlend has told me the two of them have sworn themselves to each other by the dearest oaths. Perhaps you think you possess authority to release your child, since she swore without your consent. But Erlend you cannot release. Nor can I see anything standing chiefly in the way except your pride—and your hatred of sin. But in that matter it seems to me that you would be stricter than God himself, Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn!”
> Lavrans answered somewhat uncertainly:
> “Perhaps there is some truth in what you tell me here, Sir Baard. But chiefly I have opposed this because it seemed to me that Erlend was an unsafe man into whose hands to give my daughter.”
> “I think I dare stand surety for my foster son now,” said Baard quietly. “I know that he holds Kristin so dear that if you give her to him, he will conduct himself so that you will have no cause to complain of your son-in-law.”
> Lavrans did not answer at once. Then Sir Baard said urgently, holding out his hand:
> “In God’s name, Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn, give your consent!”
> Lavrans placed his hand in Sir Baard’s:
> “In God’s name!”
> Ragnfrid and Kristin were summoned to the loft, and Lavrans told them his decision. Sir Baard greeted the two women courteously; Sir Munan shook hands with Ragnfrid and spoke civilly to the mistress, but he greeted Kristin in the foreign fashion with a kiss, and he took his time over that greeting. Kristin felt her father watching her during it.
> “How do you like your new kinsman, Sir Munan?” he asked mockingly when he was alone with her for a moment later that evening.
> Kristin looked pleadingly at her father. Then he stroked her face several times and said no more.
> When Sir Baard and Sir Munan went to rest, the latter said:
> “I would give no small amount to see the look on this Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn’s face if he learned the truth about this precious daughter. Here you and I have had to beg on our knees for Erlend to receive as wife a woman whom he has had with him in Brynhild’s house many times—”
> “Keep your mouth shut about that,” answered Sir Baard bitterly. “The worst thing Erlend has done was enticing this child to such places—and never let Lavrans get wind of it; it is best for everyone that those two can now become friends.”
> It was agreed that the betrothal ale should be drunk that same autumn. Lavrans said he could not make the feast very large because the previous year had been a famine year in the valley; in return he would himself bear the cost of the wedding and hold it at Jørundgaard with seemly splendor. He again cited the famine as his reason for demanding that the betrothal last one year.
> 
> VI.
> 
> The betrothal feast was postponed for various reasons; it was not held until after New Year, but Lavrans agreed that the wedding should not therefore be delayed; it was to take place immediately after Michaelmas, as the first agreement had stipulated.
> Thus Kristin sat at Jørundgaard as Erlend’s lawful betrothed. Together with her mother she looked over all the furnishings that had been gathered for her and labored to increase still further the piles of bedding and clothes, for Lavrans wished nothing to be spared now that he had given his daughter to the master of Husaby.
> Kristin herself wondered that she was not happier now. But despite all the bustle there was no true joy at Jørundgaard.
> Her parents sorely missed Ulvhild; that she understood. But she also understood that this alone did not cause them to be so quiet and unhappy. They were kind to her, but when they spoke to her of her betrothed, she understood that they made an effort and did it to please her and show her kindness; they did not do it because they themselves wished to speak of Erlend. They had not grown happier about the marriage she was making now that they had become acquainted with the man. Erlend himself had also been quiet and withdrawn during the short time he had stayed at Jørundgaard for the betrothal—and it could scarcely have been otherwise, Kristin thought; he knew that her father had given his consent only reluctantly.
> She and Erlend had scarcely exchanged ten words in private. It had been strange and unfamiliar for them both to sit together thus in everyone’s sight; then they had had little to speak of because they shared so much in secret. A vague and obscure fear dawned within her, uncertain but always present—that somehow it might be difficult for them when they were married because first they had stood far too close to each other, and afterward had been wholly separated for far too long.
> But she tried to put it from her. Erlend intended to visit them at Jørundgaard around Whitsuntide; he had asked Lavrans and Ragnfrid whether they would object to his coming, and Lavrans had laughed a little and answered that Erlend should know he would receive his son-in-law well.
> Around Whitsuntide they could walk outdoors together; then they could speak together as in former days, and then this shadow that had come between them through the long separation—when each had gone alone and borne his burden alone—would surely pass.
> At Easter Simon Andressøn and his wife were at Formo. Kristin saw them in church. Simon’s wife stood not far from her.
> She must be much older than he, Kristin thought—nearly thirty years. Lady Halfrid was slight, small, and thin, but she had a wonderfully lovely face. Even the pale-brown color of her hair, which waved out beneath the linen cloth, seemed gentle, and her eyes were full of gentleness; they were large and gray, sprinkled with tiny flecks of gold. Every feature of her face was fine and pure—but her skin was somewhat gray-pale, and when she opened her mouth one could see that her teeth were not good. She did not look strong and was said to be sickly—she had already miscarried several times, Kristin heard. She wondered how Simon fared with this wife.
> The people of Jørundgaard and those from Formo had greeted each other across the church hill a few times, but had not spoken together. On the third day, however, Simon was at church without his wife. Then he went over to Lavrans, and they spoke for a while. Kristin heard Ulvhild mentioned between them. Afterward he spoke with Ragnfrid. Ramborg, who stood by her mother, said quite loudly: “I remember you well—I know who you are.” Simon lifted the child a little and spun her around. “That was fair of you, Ramborg, not to have forgotten me.” He greeted Kristin only from some distance away. And afterward her parents did not mention the meeting.
> But Kristin thought a great deal about it. It had nevertheless been strange to see Simon Darre again as a married man. So much that was old came alive through it: she remembered her own blind and submissive love for Erlend then. Now it was somewhat different. She wondered whether Simon had told his wife how he and she had parted—but she knew he had not done so, “for my father’s sake,” she thought mockingly. It was strangely impoverishing that she still walked unmarried at home with her parents. But they were betrothed; Simon could see that they had carried their defiance through. Whatever else Erlend had done, he had held faithfully to her, and she had not been loose or wanton.
> One evening early in spring, Ragnfrid wished to send a message southward to Old Gunhild, the widow who sewed furs. The evening was so beautiful, and Kristin asked whether she might not go; at last she received permission, since all the men were busy.
> It was after sunset, and a fine white frost haze rose toward the golden-green sky. At every hoofbeat Kristin heard the brittle sound of evening ice breaking and scattering with a rustle. But from the thickets along the road the birdsong rejoiced softly and springlike into the twilight.
> Kristin rode swiftly down the valley; she did not think much of anything, but merely felt it was good to be alone outdoors again. She rode with her eyes fixed on the new moon, which was sinking toward the mountain ridge on the other side of the valley. Thus she nearly fell from the horse when it suddenly sprang sideways and reared.
> She saw a dark body lying huddled by the roadside—at first she was afraid. She could never rid herself of the ugly fear she had acquired of meeting people alone on the road. But she thought it might be a traveler who had fallen ill, so once she had regained control of the horse she turned it and rode back, calling to ask whether anyone was there.
> The bundle moved a little, and a voice said:
> “I believe it is you yourself, Kristin Lavransdatter—”
> “Brother Edvin?” she asked softly. She was close to believing this was an illusion or devilry meant to deceive her. But she went over to him; it truly was the old man, and he could not rise without help.
> “My dear father—are you out wandering at this season of the year?” she said in amazement.
> “Praised be God who sent you this way tonight,” said the friar. Kristin noticed that he trembled throughout his body. “I intended to go north to you, but now I could walk no farther tonight. I almost thought it was God’s will that I should lie and die on the roads where I have lain and roamed all my life. But I should have liked to receive confession and the last rites. And I should have liked to see you once more, my daughter—”
> Kristin helped the friar onto her horse; she led it by the bridle and supported him. Between lamenting that she was getting her feet wet in the slushy ice, he moaned softly with pain.
> He told her he had been at Eyabu since Christmas; some wealthy farmers in the district had vowed during the famine to improve their church with new adornments. But the work had progressed slowly; he had been ill through the winter—there was some affliction in his stomach, so that he vomited blood and could not bear food. He himself believed he did not have long to live, and he now longed home to his monastery, preferring to die there among his brothers. But first he had conceived a desire to travel northward through the valley one last time, and so he had accompanied the priest-monk from Hamar who traveled north to become the new master of the pilgrims’ hospice at Roaldstad. From Fron he had continued alone.
> “I heard that you had become betrothed,” he said, “to this man—. And then I felt such longing to see you. It grieved me deeply that our last meeting should have been that one outside our church. It has lain so heavily upon my heart, Kristin, that you had entered upon the path of discord—”
> Kristin kissed the friar’s hand and said:
> “I do not understand, Father, what I have done or deserved that you show me such great love.”
> The friar answered softly:
> “I have often thought, Kristin, that if matters had come about so that we met more often, you might have become my spiritual daughter.”
> “Do you mean that then you would have led me to turn my mind toward convent life?” asked Kristin. A little later she said: “Sira Eirik laid it upon me that if I could not obtain my father’s consent and marry Erlend, then I must enter a devout sisterhood and make amends for my sins—”
> “I have often prayed that you might feel longing for convent life,” said Brother Edvin. “But not since you said what you know. I wanted you to come to God with your maiden’s wreath, Kristin—”
> When they reached Jørundgaard, Brother Edvin had to be carried inside and put to bed. They laid him in the old winter room, the hearth-room, and tended him as well as they could. He was very ill, and Sira Eirik came over and attended him with medicines for body and soul. But the priest said it was cancer that afflicted the old man and that he could not have long to live. Brother Edvin himself thought that once he had recovered a little strength, he would travel south again and try to reach home to his monastery. Sira Eirik told the others he did not think this possible.
> Everyone at Jørundgaard felt that a great peace and joy had come among them with the friar. People came and went in the hearth-room all day, and there was never a lack of someone willing to keep watch at night beside the sick man. As many as had time gathered to sit and listen when Sira Eirik came over and read to the dying man from devotional books, and when they spoke with Brother Edvin about spiritual things. And although much of what he said was dark and obscure, as he had always been accustomed to speak, people felt that he strengthened and comforted their souls, because each person could understand that Brother Edvin was wholly filled with love of God.
> But the friar also wished to hear about every other sort of thing, asked for news from the districts, and had Lavrans tell him of the famine. There were people who in their distress had resorted to evil means and turned to helpers whom Christian men must shun. A little way up the ridge west of the valley there was a place in the mountain with some great white stones shaped like people’s private parts, and some men had fallen to sacrificing boars and he-goats before this abomination. Sira Eirik had then taken some of the most devout and courageous farmers there one night, and they had broken the stones to pieces. Lavrans had been with them and could testify that they were wholly smeared with blood, and bones and such things lay around them.—Up in Heidal, people were said to have made an old woman sit outdoors upon an earth-fast stone and chant ancient spells on three Thursday nights.
> One night Kristin sat alone with Brother Edvin.
> Around midnight he awoke and seemed to suffer great pain. Then he asked Kristin to take the book about the Virgin Mary’s miracles, which Sira Eirik had lent Brother Edvin, and read to him.
> Kristin was unaccustomed to reading aloud, but she sat on the bed-step and placed the candle beside her; she took the book on her lap and read as well as she could.
> After a while she saw the sick man lying with his teeth clenched; he knotted his wasted hands during the attacks of pain.
> “You suffer greatly, dear Father,” said Kristin sorrowfully.
> “So it seems to me now. But I know it is God who has made me a child again and tosses me up and down—.
> “I remember once when I was small—I was four winters old—I had slipped away from home and into the forest. I lost my way there and was out for many nights and days—. My mother was among the people who found me, and when she took me up in her arms, she bit me in the neck, I remember. I thought it was because she was angry with me, but later I understood better.
> “Now I myself long home from this forest. It is written: leave all things and follow me; but there has been far too much here in the world that I had no desire to leave—”
> “You, Father?” said Kristin. “I have always heard everyone say that you have been an example of chastity and poverty and humility—”
> The friar chuckled:
> “Yes, you young child surely think there is nothing in the world that tempts except sensual pleasure and riches and power. I tell you, these are small things one finds by the roadside; and I—I loved the roads—not the world’s small things did I love, but the whole world. God gave me grace to love Lady Poverty and Lady Chastity from my youth, and therefore I thought that with these play-sisters I went safely; and so I wandered and walked, and wished I could travel every road on earth. And my heart and thoughts wandered and walked too—I fear I often lost my way in my thoughts about the darkest matters. But now it is over, little Kristin; now I wish to go home to my house and lay aside all my own thoughts, and hear the guardian’s clear words about what I am to believe and think concerning my sin and the grace of God—”
> A little later he fell asleep. Kristin sat by the hearth and tended the fire. But toward morning, when she herself was about to doze, Brother Edvin suddenly spoke from the bed:
> “I am glad, Kristin, that this matter between Erlend Nikulaussøn and you has been brought to a good end.”
> Then Kristin burst into tears:
> “We have committed so much wrong before reaching this point. And what gnaws most at my heart is that I have caused my father so much sorrow. He is not happy about it now either. And yet he does not know—if he knew everything, he would surely withdraw his friendship from me entirely.”
> “Kristin,” said Brother Edvin gently, “do you not understand, child, that this is why you must keep silent before him, and why you must not cause him further sorrow—because he will never demand atonement from you? Nothing you could do could change your father’s disposition toward you.”
> A few days later Brother Edvin felt well enough to wish to travel south. Since this lay so greatly upon his heart, Lavrans had a kind of litter made, suspended between two horses, and thus he carried the sick man as far south as Lidstad; there Brother Edvin received fresh horses and a new escort, and in this way he was carried all the way to Hamar. There he died in the monastery of the preaching friars and was buried in their church. Afterward the barefoot friars demanded that the body be surrendered to them, because many people in the surrounding districts held him to be a holy man and spoke of him as Saint Even; the farmers prayed to him throughout the Uplands and the Valleys, all the way north to Trondheim. There was therefore a long dispute between the two monasteries over the body.
> Kristin heard of this only much later. But she grieved sorely when she parted from the friar. It seemed to her that he alone fully knew her life—he had known the innocent child she had been under her father’s protection. And he had known of her secret life with Erlend, so that he was like a clasp, she thought, binding together all she had held dear with what now filled her mind. Now she was wholly separated from herself as she had been when she was a maiden.
> 
> VII.
> 
> “I think,” said Ragnfrid, feeling the lukewarm brew in the vessels, “that it has now cooled enough for us to mix in the yeast.”
> Kristin had sat in the brewhouse doorway spinning while she waited for the wort to cool. She laid the spindle on the doorstep, unwrapped the pail of dissolved yeast from the coverlet, and measured it out.
> “Close the door first,” her mother asked, “so no draft comes in.—You walk as though asleep, Kristin,” she said irritably.
> Kristin strained the yeast into the brewing vessels while Ragnfrid stirred.
> Geirhild Drivsdatter called upon Hatt, but it was Odin. Then he came and helped her with the brewing; as his payment he demanded what lay between the vessel and her.—It was a saga Lavrans had once told when she was little.
> What lay between the vessel and her.—Kristin felt sick and dizzy from the heat and the sweet, spicy vapor in the dark, closed brewhouse.
> Out in the yard Ramborg walked in a ring with a group of children and sang:
> 
> The eagle sits on the highest hall
> and crooks his golden claw—
> 
> Kristin followed her mother out through the small anteroom, where empty ale casks and all kinds of tools lay. From there a door led to a strip of ground between the rear wall of the brewhouse and the rail fence around the barley field. A flock of pigs shoved one another, bit, and squealed as they fought over the warm spent grain that had been thrown out.
> Kristin shaded her eyes with her hand against the dazzling midday sunlight. Her mother looked at the pigs and said:
> “We cannot manage with fewer than eighteen reindeer.”
> “Can we need so many, do you think?” said her daughter distractedly.
> “Yes, we must serve game with the pork every day,” answered her mother. “And of birds and hare we shall scarcely get more than will be consumed in the upper loft alone. You must remember that close to two hundred people are coming—with servants and children—and the poor who must be fed. And even if you and Erlend depart on the fifth day, some of the guests will surely remain through the week—at least.”
> “You must stay here and watch the ale, Kristin,” said Ragnfrid. “I must go and provide food for your father and the haymakers.”
> Kristin fetched her spinning and sat in the rear doorway. She tucked the distaff with its head of wool into her armpit, but her hands sank into her lap with the spindle.
> Beyond the rail fence the barley ears glinted like silver and silk in the sun. Above the river’s rushing she heard now and then the sound of scythes in the meadows on the island—sometimes the iron struck a stone. Her father and the household were toiling to finish the haymaking. There was so much to struggle with for her wedding.
> The smell of the warm spent grain and the sharp stench of the pigs—she grew nauseated again. And the midday heat made her so dizzy and faint. White-faced and rigid in the back, she sat waiting for it to pass—she did not wish to be sick again.
> She had never felt like this before. It was useless to try to comfort herself that it was not yet certain, that she might be mistaken. What lay between the vessel and her—.
> Eighteen reindeer. Close to two hundred wedding guests.—People would have something to laugh at when it became known that all this commotion had been merely about a woman with child whom they must see married in time—.
> Oh no. She threw down her spinning and sprang up.
> With her forehead against the brewhouse wall, she retched into the nettle thicket growing lushly beside it. Brown caterpillars swarmed on the nettles—seeing them made her still sicker.
> Kristin passed her hands over her sweat-wet temples.
> Oh no, it was certain enough—.
> They were to be married on the second Sunday after Michaelmas, and then their wedding was to be celebrated for five days. More than two months remained. By then they might see it upon her—her mother and the other wives in the district. They were always so wise in such matters; they always knew when a woman went with child months before Kristin could understand what they saw it from. Poor thing, how she fades—. Impatiently Kristin rubbed her hands against her cheeks, for she felt they were bloodlessly white.
> Formerly she had so often thought that this must surely happen sometime. And she had not been so terribly afraid of it. But it would not have been the same then, when they could not and might not have one another lawfully. It was counted—yes, as shame in a manner, and as sin too—but if there were two young people who would not let themselves be forced apart, people did not forget that and spoke of them with kindness. She would not have been ashamed. But when such a thing happened between betrothed people—it was merely laughed at and made the subject of coarse jests. She herself understood—it was ridiculous; here they were brewing and blending wine, slaughtering, baking, and preparing for a wedding that people would speak of—and she, the bride, grew nauseated merely from smelling food, crept cold-sweating behind the outbuildings, and was sick—.
> Erlend. She clenched her teeth in anger. He should have spared her this. For she had not wished it. He should have remembered that formerly, when everything had been so uncertain for her and she had possessed nothing to rely upon except his love, she had always, always gladly yielded to his will. He should have left her alone now when she tried to refuse, because she thought it was not fair of them to steal anything for themselves after her father had joined their hands before both their kindreds. But he had taken her, half by force, and with laughter and caresses, so that she had been unable to show him that she had meant her resistance seriously.
> She went inside and looked to the ale, came back, and leaned over the rail fence. The grain swayed faintly in a little breath of air and gleamed. She could not remember ever seeing the fields so dense and luxuriant as this year.—The river flashed far away, and she heard her father’s voice calling—she could not distinguish the words, but the people out on the island laughed.
> Should she go to her father and tell him?—It would be better to abandon all this labor and join her and Erlend quietly, without a church wedding or great feast—now that the only matter was for her to receive the name of wife before it became visible to everyone that she already carried Erlend’s child beneath her breast.
> Erlend too would become an object of mockery, as much as she herself—or more; he was no young boy. But it was he who had wanted this wedding; he had wanted to see her stand as a bride in silk and velvet and a high golden crown—he had wanted that, and he had wanted to possess her too in all the sweet, secret moments. She had yielded to him in everything. She would continue to do his will in the other matter too.
> Then in the end he would see—no one could have both things. He who had spoken of the great Christmas feast he would hold at Husaby during her first year as mistress on his farm—then he would display to all his kinsmen, friends, and people from the districts far around the beautiful wife he had gained. Kristin smiled mockingly. This would scarcely be fitting around Christmas this year.
> Around Saint Gregory’s Mass it would be. It was as though her thoughts began to swarm in her mind when she told herself that around Saint Gregory’s Mass she would give birth. She was somewhat afraid of that too—she remembered her mother’s screams ringing over the farm for two days when Ulvhild came into the world. At Ulvsvolden two young wives in succession had died in childbirth—and both of Sigurd of Loptsgaard’s first wives. And her own paternal grandmother, after whom she was named—.
> But she was not chiefly afraid. During these years she had often thought, whenever she noticed anew that she was still not pregnant, that perhaps this would be the punishment upon her and Erlend: that she would continue barren. They would come to wait and wait in vain for what they had formerly feared; they would come to hope as vainly as they had formerly feared without need. Until at last they knew that one day they would be carried out from his ancestral estate and be gone—his brother was a priest, and the children he had could never inherit him. Munan Stump and his sons would enter and sit in their seat, and Erlend would be erased from the lineage.
> She pressed a hand hard against her lap. It was there—between the rail fence and her, between the vessel and her. Between her and the whole world it was—Erlend’s lawful son. She had already tried the means she had once heard Lady Aashild speak of, with blood from the right and left arm. It was a son she carried. Whatever he should bring her.—She remembered her dead little brothers, her parents’ sorrowful faces on the occasions when they mentioned them; she remembered all the times she had seen them despair for Ulvhild’s sake—the night Ulvhild died. And she thought of all the sorrow she herself had caused them, of her father’s grief-worn face—and still this was not the end of the sorrows she would bring upon Father and Mother—.
> Nevertheless, nevertheless. Kristin laid her head upon her arm over the rail fence; her other hand she still held over her lap. If it brought her new sorrows, if it should force her to death—she would rather die to bear Erlend a son than that they should both die one day and the houses stand empty after them, and grain sway for strangers over their fields—.
> Someone entered the anteroom. The ale! thought Kristin—I should have looked to it long ago. She straightened herself—then Erlend ducked beneath the doorframe and came out into the sunlight, radiantly glad:
> “Is this where you stand?” he said. “And you do not even come one step to meet me?” he asked; he came over and put his arms around her.
> “Dear one, have you come here?” she said in surprise.
> He must just have dismounted—he still had his cloak over his shoulder and his sword at his side—was disheveled, dirty, and very dusty. He wore a red tunic that fell in folds from the neckline and was slit up the sides almost to beneath the arms. As they went through the brewhouse and up the yard, the garment flapped around him so that his thighs were visible all the way to the waist. It was strange—she had never before noticed that he turned his feet slightly outward when he walked—formerly she had seen only that he had long, slender legs, fine ankles, and small, well-shaped feet.
> Erlend had come with a large company—with five servants and four spare horses. He told Ragnfrid he had come to fetch Kristin’s goods—surely it would be more pleasant for her to find her things at Husaby when she arrived there? And so late in autumn as the wedding was to be held, it might become harder to transport them—and they could easily be spoiled by seawater aboard ship. Now the abbot of Nidarholm had offered to let him send them on the Laurentius vessel—they expected it to sail from Veøy around the Feast of the Assumption. Therefore he had come to have the goods driven through Raumsdal to the headland.
> He sat in the cookhouse doorway drinking ale and talking while Ragnfrid and Kristin plucked the wild ducks Lavrans had brought home the day before. Mother and daughter were alone near the buildings; the women were in the meadow raking. He looked so happy—well pleased with himself for having come on so sensible an errand.
> Her mother went out, and Kristin tended the spit with the birds. Through the open door she could glimpse the group of servants lying in the shade on the other side of the yard and passing the ale bowl among themselves. Erlend himself sat on the threshold, chatting and laughing—the sun shone directly on his bare, soot-black hair; she discovered that there were some gray strands in it. Yes, he must soon be thirty-two—but he behaved like a frolicsome boy. She knew she would not tell him of her difficulty—soon enough, when he saw it for himself. Tenderness full of laughter streamed through her mind over the small hard anger at the bottom, like a glittering river over stones.
> She loved him beyond everything—it filled her mind, although all the while she saw and remembered everything else. How poorly this courtly man in the fine red tunic, with silver spurs on his feet and a gold-adorned belt, fitted into the harvest bustle here at Jørundgaard.—She also noticed that her father did not come up to the farm, although her mother had sent Ramborg down to the river with word of what guest had arrived.
> Erlend stood beside her and put his hands around her shoulders:
> “Can you understand it?” he said radiantly. “Does it not seem strange to you—that it is our wedding all this labor is for?”
> Kristin gave him a kiss and pushed him away—ladled fat over the birds and asked him not to stand in the way. No, she would not tell him—.
> Lavrans did not come up to the farm until suppertime—together with the haymakers. He was dressed scarcely differently from the laborers, in an undyed knee-length homespun tunic and ankle-length hose-trousers of the same cloth; he went barefoot and carried the scythe over his shoulder. The only thing distinguishing his dress from the servants’ was a leather shoulder-piece for the falcon perched on his left shoulder. He led Ramborg by the hand.
> Lavrans greeted his son-in-law cordially enough and asked him to excuse his not having come sooner—they had to drive themselves as hard as they could with the farm work, for he himself was obliged to make a journey to town between the haymaking and the grain harvest. But when Erlend stated his errand over the meal, Lavrans became rather curt.
> He could not possibly spare carts and horses now. Erlend answered that he himself had brought four spare horses. Lavrans thought there would be at least three loads. Besides, the maiden must keep her everyday clothes here. And they needed the bedding Kristin was to take with her on the farm during the wedding, with so many guests to lodge.
> No, no, Erlend conceded. Surely some means could be found to transport it in autumn too. But he had been pleased and thought it sounded sensible when the abbot proposed that it might go on the monastery vessel. The abbot had recalled their kinship: “They all remember it now,” said Erlend, smiling. His father-in-law’s displeasure did not seem to affect him in the least.
> It was nevertheless settled that Erlend should borrow one cart and take away a load of the things Kristin might need most immediately upon coming to her new home.
> The next day they were busy packing. Her mother thought the large and small looms could go now—Kristin would scarcely have time to weave more before the wedding. Ragnfrid and her daughter cut down the cloth that was on the loom. It was undyed homespun, but made from the finest, softest wool, with tufts spun in from black sheep to form regular speckles. Kristin and her mother rolled up the cloth and put it in the leather sack. Kristin thought it would be good for swaddling—beautiful too, when red or blue bands were wound over it.
> The sewing stand Arne had once forged could also be sent. Kristin took from the casket all the things Erlend had given her from time to time. She showed her mother the blue velvet cloak with a red pattern that she was to wear on the bridal journey. Her mother turned it over and over and felt the cloth and fur lining.
> “It was a very costly cloak,” said Ragnfrid. “When did Erlend give you this?”
> “He gave it to me while I was at Nonneseter,” said her daughter.
> Kristin’s bridal chest, which her mother had been filling since Kristin was small, was repacked. It was carved into squares, with a leaping animal or bird among leaves in each panel. Ragnfrid put her bridal gown in one of her own chests. It was not entirely finished; they had sewn on it during the winter. It was of scarlet silk and cut to fit very tightly around the body. Kristin thought that now it would be far too tight across her breasts.
> Toward evening the load stood ready, strapped beneath the cart’s covering. Erlend was to depart early the next morning.
> He stood with Kristin leaning over the farm gate, looking northward, where a blue-black darkness of storm filled the valley. Thunder rolled within the mountain—but southward the fields and river lay in yellow, piercing sunlight.
> “Do you remember the storm that day in the forest by Gerdarud?” he asked softly, playing with her fingers.
> Kristin nodded and tried to smile. The air was so heavy and sultry—her head ached, and she sweated with every breath she took.
> Lavrans came over to the two by the gate and spoke of the weather. It seldom did damage down here in the district—but God must know whether they would hear of disaster to the cattle and horses up in the mountains.
> It was black as night above the church on the hill. A flash of lightning revealed a herd of horses standing crowded and restless on the field outside the churchyard gate. Lavrans did not think they belonged here in the district—they were more likely horses from Dovre that had grazed in the mountains beneath Jetta; nevertheless he had a mind to go up and look at them, he shouted through the thunder—in case any of his should be among them—.
> A terrible flash of lightning tore open the darkness above—the thunder rattled and crashed so that they could hear no earthly sound. The herd of horses galloped across the fields below the ridge. All three crossed themselves—
> Then came another bolt; it was as though the sky split directly overhead, and a vast snow-white flame of fire swept down upon them—the three were thrown against each other, stood with closed and blinded eyes, and smelled something like scorched stone—and the thunderclap made their ears deaf.
> “Saint Olav, help us,” said Lavrans softly. “Look at the birch, look at the birch!” screamed Erlend: the great birch inside the field seemed to totter—then a huge branch separated and sank toward the ground, tearing a gash from the trunk.
> “Do you think it will catch fire—Jesus Christ! The church roof is burning,” cried Lavrans.
> They stood staring—no—yes! Red flames seeped forth in the shingle covering beneath the roof turret.
> Both men ran back across the yard. Lavrans tore open every house door and shouted inside; people swarmed out.
> “Take axes, take axes—the timber axes,” he cried, “and hooks—” He raced to the stable. Immediately afterward he emerged leading Guldsveinen by the mane, sprang upon the unsaddled horse, and galloped northward; he held the great broad-axe in his hand. Erlend rode directly after him—all the men followed; some were mounted, but some could not control the frightened animals, gave up, and ran after them. Last came Ragnfrid and the women of the farm with buckets and pails.
> No one seemed any longer to heed the storm. In the lightning they saw people streaming from the houses farther down the district. Sira Eirik was already running up the hill, followed by his household. Horse hooves thundered on the bridge below—some men galloped past; they turned white, terrified faces toward their burning church.
> A slight wind blew from the southeast. The fire had taken good hold on the north wall; in the west the entrance door was already blocked. But it had not yet caught the south side or the rounded end of the chancel.
