> Kristin and the women from Jørundgaard entered the graveyard south of the church at a place where the fence had been torn down.
> The mighty red glow illuminated the grove north of the church and the place with the rails for tethering horses. No one could approach there because of the heat—the cross stood alone in front, bathed in the light of the flames. It looked as though it lived and moved.
> Through the roaring and shrieking of the fire sounded the blows of axes against the south wall’s staves. Men in the gallery hacked and hammered, while others tried to tear down the covered walkway itself. Someone called to the women from Jørundgaard that Lavrans and a few other men had followed Sira Eirik into the church. They must try to make an opening in the wall—small tongues of fire were playing among the shingles over the roof here too. If the wind shifted or died, the flames would encompass the whole church.
> To think of extinguishing it was useless; there was no time to form a chain down to the river, but at Ragnfrid’s command the women lined up and passed water from the little brook running west beside the road—it was enough to throw a little upon the south wall and upon the men working there. Many of them sobbed as they worked, from tension and fear for the people who had entered the burning building, and from sorrow for their church.
> Kristin stood at the front of the line of women and passed buckets—she stared breathlessly at the church where both her father and surely Erlend were inside.
> The columns of the gallery lay torn down in a tangle of wood and shingles from the roof of the walkway. The men attacked the stave wall with all their might—a whole group had lifted a log and charged against it.
> Erlend and one of his servants emerged from the small door south of the chancel; between them they carried the great chest from the sacristy—the one on which Eirik usually sat when hearing confession. Erlend and the man overturned the chest onto the churchyard.
> Kristin could not hear what he shouted; he ran back, up into the gallery again. He was supple as a cat as he ran there—he had cast off his outer clothes and ran in only shirt, breeches, and hose.
> The others took up his cry—the sacristy and chancel were burning; no one could come from the nave to the south door any longer—the fire now blocked both exits. A pair of staves in the wall had been splintered; Erlend had taken a fire hook and tore and pulled at the wreckage of the staves—they stripped a hole in the side of the church while other people shouted that they must take care: the roof might fall and imprison those inside; the shingled roof was now burning briskly on this side too, and the heat was becoming unbearable.
> Erlend sprang into the hole and helped Sira Eirik out. The priest came with the skirts of his robe full of the holy vessels from the altars.
> A young boy followed with one hand over his face and the shaft of the tall processional cross thrust before him. Lavrans came after. He held his eyes shut against the smoke—he staggered beneath the huge crucifix he carried in his arms; it was much taller than the man.
> People ran forward and helped them down into the churchyard. Sira Eirik stumbled, fell to his knees, and the altar vessels rolled down the hill. The silver dove sprang open and the Host fell out—the priest picked it up, brushed it off, and kissed it while sobbing aloud; he kissed the gilded man’s head that had stood over the altar with some of Saint Olav’s hair and nails inside it.
> Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn still stood holding the rood. His arm lay along the cross’s arms, and he inclined his head toward the shoulder of the image of Christ; it looked as though the Savior bent his fair, sorrowful face consolingly toward the man.
> The roof had begun to fall inward piece by piece on the north side of the church—a burning length of beam was flung outward and struck the great bell in the bell frame by the churchyard gate. The bell sounded with a deep sobbing note that died in a long groan drowned by the roaring fire.
> No one had paid heed to the weather during this—the whole thing had not lasted long, but people had not been conscious of that either. Now thunder and lightning had moved far south in the valley; the rain, which had been falling for a while, increased, and the wind subsided.
> But suddenly it was as though a sail of flame were hoisted from the sill—one instant, and with a howl the fire embraced the church from end to end.
> People fled from the devouring heat. Erlend was suddenly at Kristin’s side and pulled her down the hill. His whole body smelled scorched—her hand came away full of burned hair when she stroked his head and face. They could not hear each other’s voices through the roaring fire. But she saw that his eyebrows had been singed away to the roots, that he had burns on his face, and that there were burned places in his shirt. He laughed as he pulled her with him after the others.
> The people followed the weeping old priest and Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn with the crucifix.
> At the end of the churchyard Lavrans leaned the rood against a tree and sank down on the wreckage of the fence. Sira Eirik was already sitting there—he stretched out his arms toward the burning church:
> “Farewell, farewell, Church of Olav. God bless you, my Church of Olav; God bless you for every hour I have sung and celebrated Mass within you—Church of Olav, good night, good night—”
> The parishioners wept aloud with him. Rain streamed down over the groups of people, but no one thought of leaving. It did not seem to dampen the heat in the tarred woodwork—burning brands and glowing shingles were flung about. Immediately afterward the roof turret collapsed into the fire with a cloud of sparks rising skyward behind it.
> Lavrans sat with one hand over his face; his other arm lay across his lap, and Kristin saw that the sleeve was bloody from the shoulder all the way down; blood ran over his fingers. She went over and touched him.
> “It is surely not much—something fell on my shoulder,” he said and looked up. He was white to the lips. “Ulvhild,” he whispered in anguish, looking into the blaze.
> Sira Eirik heard and laid a hand on his shoulder.
> “It does not wake your child, Lavrans—she sleeps just as well though the place above her bed burns,” he said. “She has not lost the home of her soul, as the rest of us have tonight.”
> Kristin hid her face against Erlend’s breast—stood so and felt his arms around her shoulders. Then she heard her father ask for his wife.
> Someone answered that a woman had gone into labor from fright; they had carried her down to the rectory. And Ragnfrid had gone with her.
> Kristin remembered again what she had entirely forgotten from the moment they realized the church was burning. She surely should not have looked upon this. There was a man south in the district with a red mark over half his face; they said he had become so because his mother had looked at wildfire while carrying him.
> Dear holy Virgin Mary, she prayed within herself, do not let my unborn child have suffered harm from this—.
> The next day a district assembly was summoned on the church hill—the people were to deliberate on rebuilding the church.
> Kristin sought Sira Eirik at Romundgaard before he went to the assembly. She asked the priest whether he thought she ought to take this as a sign. Perhaps it was God’s will that she tell her father she was unworthy to stand beneath the bridal crown; it would be more seemly for her to be given to Erlend Nikulaussøn in marriage without a feast of honor.
> But Sira Eirik sprang up against her, his eyes blazing with anger:
> “Do you think God cares so much how you bitches run about and squander yourselves that he would burn down a fair and honorable church for your sake! Put away your pride, and do not cause your mother and Lavrans a sorrow they will be long in overcoming. If you do not bear the crown with honor on your day of honor—that is ill for you; but all the more do you and Erlend need the consecration when you are joined. Each person has his sins to answer for; therefore this misfortune has surely come upon us all. See to amending your life, and help rebuild the church here, both you and Erlend.”
> Kristin thought she had not yet told him of this last thing that had happened—but she resigned herself to his answer.
> She went with the men to the assembly. Lavrans came with his arm in a sling, and Erlend had many burns upon his face; he looked dreadful, but he laughed at it. None of the wounds was large, and he said he hoped they would not disfigure him when he was to be a bridegroom. He stood after Lavrans and promised to offer the church four marks of silver and, on behalf of his betrothed and with Lavrans’s consent, a mark’s worth of Kristin’s land in the parish.
> Erlend had to remain at Jørundgaard for a week because of his wounds. Kristin saw that Lavrans seemed to have come to like his son-in-law better after the night of the fire; the men now appeared to be good friends. She then thought that perhaps her father might grow so fond of Erlend Nikulaussøn that he would judge indulgently and not take it as heavily as she had feared when the time came that he understood how they had offended against him.
> 
> VIII.
> 
> That year was unusually good throughout the northern valley. There was much hay and it was brought in dry; from the mountain pastures the people returned with an abundance of dairy produce and fat herds—and they had been mercifully spared wild beasts that year. The grain stood so beautifully that few could remember ever seeing it better—it ripened well and was full of substance, and the weather was the very best. Between Saint Bartholomew’s Mass and the Nativity of Mary, when night frosts were most to be feared, there was a little rain and mild, cloudy weather; afterward the harvest month passed with sun and wind and mild, misty nights. In the week after Michaelmas, most of the grain had been brought in throughout the district.
> At Jørundgaard they labored and prepared for the great wedding. During the last two months Kristin had been so busy from morning to evening every single day that she had little time to trouble herself about anything but the work. She saw that she had grown large in the breasts; the small pale-pink nipples turned brown and were tender as sores every morning when she had to rise into the cold—but that passed once she had worked herself warm, and then she thought only of all she must accomplish before evening. When she had to straighten her back now and then and stand resting, she felt that what she bore in her lap was beginning to grow heavy—but she still looked as narrow and slender as before. She ran her hands down her long, fine hips. No, she would not grieve over it now. It sometimes came over her that she thought with a little tingling longing—in a month or so she would surely feel life—. By then she would be at Husaby. Perhaps Erlend would be glad—.
> She closed her eyes and bit upon her betrothal ring—saw before her Erlend’s face, pale with emotion, when he stood in the upper loft and spoke the words of betrothal in a loud, clear voice:
> “So let God be my witness and these men who stand here, that I, Erlend Nikulaussøn, lawfully bind myself to Kristin Lavransdatter according to God’s law and man’s law, upon such conditions as have here been spoken before these witnesses who stand by. That I shall possess you as my wife and you shall possess me as your husband, as long as we both live, to dwell together in wedlock, with all such fellowship as God’s law and the law of the land recognize.”
> She ran on errands across the yard from house to house and stopped for a moment—the rowan was so full of berries that year; it would be a snowy winter. And the sun shone over the pale fields where the grain stood on drying poles. If only this weather would hold through the wedding.
> Lavrans insisted that his daughter be married in a church. It had therefore been decided that this should take place in the chapel at Sundbu. On Saturday the bridal party would ride across the mountain to Vaage; they would spend the night at Sundbu and neighboring farms, and ride back on Sunday after the bridal Mass. That same evening after vespers, when the holy day was over, the wedding would be celebrated and Lavrans would give his daughter to Erlend. And after midnight bride and bridegroom would be escorted to bed together.
> On Friday afternoon Kristin stood in the gallery of the upper loft and watched the party riding from the north past the burned church on the hill. She strained her eyes to distinguish Erlend from the others. They must not see each other; no man was now to see her before she was brought forth in bridal dress the next day.
> Where the road turned toward Jørundgaard, a few women separated from the company. The men rode onward to Laugar Bridge; they were to sleep there that night.
> Kristin went down to meet those who were coming. She felt so tired after the bath, and her scalp was sore—her mother had used very strong lye water on her hair to make it truly bright for the next day.
> Lady Aashild Gautesdatter slid from the saddle into Lavrans’s arms. How lightly and youthfully she bears herself, thought Kristin. Her son’s wife, Sir Munan’s lady Katrin, looked almost older; she was large and stout, colorless in skin and eyes. It is strange, thought Kristin: she is ugly and he is unfaithful, and yet people say they live well together.
> There were also two daughters of Sir Baard Petersøn, one married and one unmarried. They were neither ugly nor beautiful, looked trustworthy and good, but bore themselves rather stiffly toward strangers. Lavrans thanked them courteously because they had wished to do such honor to this wedding and travel the long way so late in autumn.
> “Erlend was reared by our father when he was a boy,” said the elder, coming forward and greeting Kristin.
> Now two young men came riding into the yard at a sharp trot—they jumped from their horses and ran laughing after Kristin, who fled into the house and hid. They were Trond Gjesling’s young sons, handsome and promising boys. They brought the bridal crown from Sundbu in a casket. Trond and his wife would not accompany them to Jørundgaard until Sunday after Mass.
> Kristin had fled into the hearth-room. Lady Aashild followed, laid her hands on Kristin’s shoulders, and drew her face down to hers for a kiss.
> “I am glad I shall live to see this day,” said Lady Aashild.
> She saw how thin Kristin’s hands had become as she held them. She saw that the bride had otherwise grown lean but was high-breasted. Every feature of her face had become narrower and finer than before; her temples seemed slightly sunken in the shadow of the heavy, damp hair. Her cheeks were no longer round, and the fresh color had faded. But Kristin’s eyes had become much larger and darker.
> Lady Aashild kissed her again.
> “I see you have had much to toil over, Kristin,” she said. “I shall give you a draught tonight so that you may be rested and fresh tomorrow.”
> Kristin’s lips began to tremble.
> “Hush,” said Lady Aashild, patting her hand. “I look forward to adorning you tomorrow—no one shall have seen a more beautiful bride than you will be tomorrow.”
> Lavrans rode over to Laugar Bridge to sit at table with the guests who were there.
> The men could not praise the food enough—one would not receive better Friday fare in the richest monastery. There was rye porridge, boiled beans, white bread, and for fish they had nothing less than trout, salted and fresh, and rich dried fish.
> As the men took more ale, they became more and more excited and jested more and more coarsely with the bridegroom. All Erlend’s groomsmen were much younger than he—his own contemporaries and friends had all been married men for a long time. Now the men joked that he was so old and was to enter a bridal bed for the first time. Some of Erlend’s older kinsmen, who were still fairly sober, sat fearing at every word that the talk would now drift toward matters better left untouched. Sir Baard of Hestnæs watched Lavrans. Lavrans drank steadily, but the ale did not seem to make him happier as he sat in the high seat—his face grew more and more strained as his eyes grew more fixed. But Erlend, sitting at his father-in-law’s right hand, answered the jests merrily and laughed much; his face was red and his eyes glittered.
> Suddenly Lavrans flared up:
> “That cart, son-in-law—while I remember it, what have you done with the cart you borrowed from me this summer—”
> “Cart—?” said Erlend.
> “Do you no longer remember borrowing a cart from me this summer? God knows it was so good a cart that I shall never see a better, for I oversaw it myself when it was made here on the farm. You promised and swore—I call God to witness, and my household knows—that you promised to bring it back to me, but you have not kept that word—”
> Some of the guests called that this was nothing to speak of now, but Lavrans struck the table and swore he would know what Erlend had done with his cart.
> “Oh, it likely stands at the farm on the headland where we took a boat to Veøy,” said Erlend indifferently. “I did not think it mattered so much. You see, Father-in-law—it was a long and arduous journey with the load through the valleys, so when we reached the fjord none of my men wished to travel all that way back here with the cart and then afterward cross the mountain north to Trondheim. Then I thought it might remain there for the time being—”
> “No, may the devil move me from where I sit if I have ever heard your equal,” Lavrans burst out. “What kind of custom do you have in your house—is it you or your servants who decide where they will or will not travel—”
> Erlend shrugged:
> “It is true that much has been otherwise than it should be at home with me.—I shall have that cart sent south to you again when Kristin and I travel over there.—My dear father-in-law,” he said, smiling and holding out his hand, “you may know that now another order will come upon everything, and upon me too, when I bring Kristin home as mistress. The matter of this cart was ill. But I promise you this shall be the last time you have cause to complain of me.”
> “Dear Lavrans,” begged Baard Petersøn, “be reconciled with him over this small matter—”
> “Small matter or greater matter,” Lavrans began. But then he checked himself and shook hands with Erlend.
> Shortly afterward he departed, and the guests at Laugar Bridge sought their sleeping places.
> On Saturday before high-mass time the wives and maidens were busy in the old loft. Some prepared the bridal bed, and some finished adorning the bride.
> Ragnfrid had chosen this house as the bridal house, partly because it was the smallest loft—they could lodge many more guests in the new storehouse loft; it was the one they themselves had used as a sleeping room in summer when Kristin was little, before Lavrans had built the great dwelling-house in which they now lived both summer and winter. But the old storehouse was also almost the most beautiful building on the farm, since Lavrans had had it restored—it had been close to collapse when they moved to Jørundgaard. It was adorned with the finest wood carving both outside and within, and though the loft-room was not large, it was all the easier to furnish beautifully with hangings, coverlets, and skins.
> The bridal bed stood ready with silk-covered cushions; beautiful hangings had been hung around it like a tent; over the skins and mattresses an embroidered silk cloth had been spread. Ragnfrid and several other women were hanging woven coverlets over the wall timbers and laying cushions on the benches.
> Kristin sat in a chair with arms that had been carried up there. She wore her scarlet bridal gown. Large brooches held it together over her breast and closed the yellow silk shift at the neckline; golden bracelets gleamed upon the yellow silk sleeves. A gilded silver belt had been wound three times around her waist, and chain upon chain lay around her neck and breast, outermost her father’s old gold chain with the great reliquary cross. Her hands, lying in her lap, were heavy with rings.
> Lady Aashild stood behind her chair and brushed out the heavy golden-brown hair.
> “Tomorrow you shall wear it loose for the last time,” she said, smiling, and wound around Kristin’s head the red and green silk bands that were to support the crown. Then the women crowded around the bride.
> Ragnfrid and Gyrid of Skog took the Gjesling lineage’s great bridal crown from the table. It was wholly gilded; the points ended alternately in crosses and clover leaves, and the circlet was set with rock crystals.
> They pressed it down around the bride’s head. Ragnfrid was pale, and her hands trembled as she did so.
> Kristin rose slowly. Jesus, how heavy it was to bear all this gold and silver—. Then Lady Aashild took her by the hand and led her to a great vessel of water, while the bridesmaids flung open the door to the sunlight so that the loft would be bright.
> “Look at yourself now, Kristin,” said Lady Aashild, and Kristin bent over the vessel. She glimpsed her own face rising white from the water; it came so near that she saw the golden crown above it. Around it so many light and dark shadows moved in the reflection—there was something she was almost remembering—then it seemed she would faint—she caught hold of the vessel’s rim. Lady Aashild placed her hand upon Kristin’s and dug her nails so painfully into the flesh that she came to herself.
> Horn calls sounded down by the bridge. From the farm people called up that the bridegroom was coming with his company.
> The wives led Kristin out onto the storehouse gallery.
> The yard surged with horses in festive harness and people in holy-day clothing; it glittered and gleamed in the sun. Kristin looked out past it all, toward the valley. Her home district lay bright and still beneath a thin haze of misty blue; from the haze rose mountains gray with scree and black with forest, and the sun poured its light down into the valley’s bowl from a cloudless sky.
> She had not noticed it before, but all the leaves were off the trees; the groves shone silver-gray and bare. Only the alder scrub along the river had a little faded green at the very tops, and here and there a birch held fast to some white-yellow leaves on the ends of its branches. But the trees were nearly bare—except the rowan, which still shone with brown-red leaves around the blood-red berries. In the still, warm day an acrid autumn scent rose from the ash-colored carpet spread everywhere by fallen leaves.
> Had it not been for the rowan trees, it might have been early spring. And but for the stillness—but it was autumn stillness, so very still. Whenever the horn calls ceased, no other sound came from the district except the clinking of bells from all the stubble fields and fallows where the cattle grazed.
> The river was diminished and small; it murmured so quietly; only a few little threads of water ran between sandbanks and great stretches of white, smoothly rounded stones. No brooks roared down the slopes—it had been so dry an autumn. Yet everywhere the fields glistened wet—but it was only the dampness that seeps up from the earth in autumn, however warm the days and clear the air.
> The crowd in the yard parted and made way for the bridegroom’s company. The young groomsmen had just ridden forward; unrest arose among the women in the gallery.
> Lady Aashild stood beside the bride:
> “Bear yourself well now, Kristin,” she said. “It will not be long until you are sheltered beneath the wife’s linen.”
> Kristin nodded helplessly. She felt how terribly white her face must be.
> “I am surely far too pale a bride,” she said softly.
> “You are the most beautiful bride,” answered Aashild, “and there rides Erlend—one must search far to find two more beautiful than you.”
> Erlend himself rode forward beneath the gallery. He sprang from the horse, light and unhindered by the heavy, many-folded clothes. Kristin thought him so beautiful that pain went through her whole body.
> He was darkly dressed in an ankle-length, slit silk tunic, withered-brown in color and woven with black and white. Around his waist he wore a gold-adorned belt, and at his left hip a sword with gold on hilt and scabbard. Over his shoulders fell a heavy, dark-blue velvet cloak, and upon his black hair he wore a black French silk cap, gathered outward like wings at the sides and ending in two long tails; one had been cast across his breast from the left shoulder and backward.
> Erlend greeted his bride above, went to her horse, and stood beside it with his hand upon the saddle bow while Lavrans came up the stairs. Kristin felt strange and dizzy amid all this splendor—her father looked unfamiliar in the solemn, ankle-length garment of green velvet. But her mother was gray-pale beneath the linen in her red silk gown.
> Ragnfrid came and placed the cloak around her daughter.
> Then Lavrans took the bride’s hand and led her down to Erlend. He lifted her onto the horse and himself mounted. The two sat side by side before the bridal loft while the procession began to ride out through the farm gate. First came the priests: Sira Eirik, Sira Tormod from Ulvsvolden, and a canon from Hamar who was her father’s friend. Then came the groomsmen and bridesmaids in pairs. And now Erlend and she were to ride forward. After them came the bride’s parents, kinsmen, friends, and guests in a long line between the rail fences to the district road. For a long distance ahead the road had been strewn with clusters of rowan berries, spruce branches, and the autumn’s last white mayweed, and people stood along the way the party was to ride, greeting them with shouts.
> On Sunday just after sunset the procession of riders returned to Jørundgaard. Through the first tatters of twilight the bonfires glowed red from the wedding farm’s yard. Minstrels and musicians sang and made fiddles and drums sound while the company rode toward the warm red glow.
> Kristin nearly collapsed when Erlend lifted her from the horse before the upper-loft gallery.
> “I was so cold across the mountain,” she whispered. “I am so tired—.” She stood for a moment—when she climbed the stairs to the loft, she swayed at every step.
> In the upper-loft room the frostbitten wedding guests soon regained warmth—heat came from all the candles burning in the room; steaming food was carried about, and wine, mead, and strong ale passed around. The murmur of voices and sound of people eating droned far away in Kristin’s ears.
> She sat and could not grow truly warm. After a while her cheeks began to burn, but her feet would not thaw, and shivers ran down her back. All the heavy gold forced her forward as she sat in the high seat beside Erlend.
> Every time the bridegroom drank to her, she had to look at the red patches and spots that stood out so sharply on his face now that he began to grow hot after the ride in the cold. They were marks from the burns of that summer.
> The terrible fear had come over her the previous evening when they sat at table at Sundbu. It came when she met Bjørn Gunnarsøn’s lifeless gaze resting upon her and Erlend—the eyes neither blinked nor strayed. They had dressed Sir Bjørn in a knight’s clothes—he looked like a dead man conjured back to life.
> At night she had lain with Lady Aashild, the bridegroom’s nearest female kinswoman in the company.
> “What is the matter with you, Kristin?” Lady Aashild had said somewhat impatiently. “You must hold yourself firm to the end now and not be so utterly cast down.”
> “I am thinking,” said Kristin, shivering, “of all those whom we have hurt so that we might live to see this day—”
> “You have not had only good yourselves,” said Lady Aashild. “Not Erlend. And I think you have had it worse.”
> “I am thinking of his defenseless children,” said the bride as before. “I wonder whether they know their father celebrates his wedding today—”
> “Think of your own child,” said the lady. “Be glad that you celebrate your wedding with the man who is its father.”
> Kristin lay for a while, sinking in dizziness. It was so strange to hear mentioned what had filled her mind every single day for three months and more, though she had been unable to speak a word of it to any living soul. But it helped her only briefly.
> “I am thinking of her who had to pay with her life because she loved Erlend,” she whispered, trembling.
> “You may yourself have to pay with your life before you are six months older,” said Lady Aashild harshly. “Rejoice while you can—.
> “What am I to say to you, Kristin?” the old woman said in exasperation. “Have you wholly lost courage now? Soon enough it will be required of you that you pay for everything you have taken—fear nothing else.”
> But Kristin felt as though avalanche after avalanche moved within her mind; everything she had built since that day of terror at Haugen was torn down. In the first time, when wildly and blindly she had thought only that she would endure—day after day she would endure. And she had endured until it grew easier—and easy at last, when she cast away every thought except this: that now their wedding would finally take place, Erlend’s wedding at last.
> She and Erlend knelt together during the bridal Mass. But everything seemed an illusion—the candles, images, glittering vessels, priests in linen vestments and copes. All the people who had known her in her former life seemed like dream visions as they stood filling the church in their unfamiliar holy-day clothes. But Sir Bjørn stood against a pillar and watched them with his dead eyes, and she thought the other dead woman must have returned with him, in his arm.
> She tried to look up at the image of Saint Olav—he stood red and white and fair, leaning upon his axe and treading his own sinful human nature beneath his feet—but Sir Bjørn drew her gaze. And near him she saw Eline Ormsdatter’s dead face, watching them indifferently. They had trampled over her to reach this place—and she did not begrudge it to them.
> She had risen and shaken off all the stones Kristin had labored to lay upon the dead woman. Erlend’s wasted youth, his honor and welfare, the favor of friends, the health of his soul. The dead woman shook them from herself. He wanted me and I wanted him; you wanted him and he wanted you, said Eline. I have paid, and he must pay, and you must pay when your time comes. When sin is perfected, it brings forth death—.
> It seemed to Kristin that she knelt with Erlend upon a cold stone. He knelt with the red burned patches upon his pale face; she knelt beneath the heavy bridal crown and felt the dull, crushing weight in her womb—the burden of sin she carried. She had played and sported with her sin, measured it as in children’s play. Holy Virgin—soon the time would come when it would lie full-borne before her, look at her with living eyes, show her the brands of sin, sin’s hideous deformity, strike with hateful, twisted hands against its mother’s breast.
> When she had borne her child, when she saw the marks of her sin upon it and loved it as she had loved her sin, then the game would be played to its end.
> Kristin thought that if she screamed now so that it cut across the song and the deep chanting voices of the men—rang out over the people—would she then be rid of Eline’s face, would life return to the dead man’s eyes? But she clenched her teeth.
> Holy King Olav, I cry to you. Of all those in Heaven I beg you for help, because I know you loved God’s justice above all things. I call upon you to take charge of the innocent one in my mother’s womb. Turn God’s wrath from the innocent one; turn it upon me, amen, in the Lord’s precious name—.
> My children, said Eline, are surely innocent too, yet there is no place for them in a land where Christian people live. Your child was conceived in outlawry, as mine were. You can claim no more right for it in the land you fled than I could claim for mine—.
> Holy Olav, I beg mercy nevertheless. Beg grace for my son; take him under your protection, and I shall carry him to your church upon my bare feet; I shall carry my golden wreath in to you and lay it upon your altar if you will help me, amen—.
> Her face was rigid as stone, so fiercely did she struggle to keep calm; but her body twitched and trembled as she knelt and was married to Erlend.
> And now she sat beside him in the high seat at home and perceived everything around her only as a mirage in feverish delirium.
> There were the minstrels playing harps and fiddles in the loft; playing and singing came from the room below and from the yard. A glow of red fire came from outside whenever people passed through the door carrying things in and out.
> They stood around the table; she stood between her father and Erlend. Her father proclaimed in a loud voice that he had now given his daughter Kristin to Erlend Nikulaussøn as wife. Erlend thanked his father-in-law and thanked all the good people who had gathered to honor him and his wife.
> They told her to sit, and Erlend laid his bridal gifts in her lap. Sira Eirik and Sir Munan Baardsøn unrolled documents and read aloud concerning their household settlement. Meanwhile the groomsmen stood with spears in their hands and struck the shafts against the floor from time to time during the reading and when gifts and purses of money were placed upon the table.
> The removable tables were cleared away; Erlend led her onto the floor, and they danced. Kristin thought: our groomsmen and bridesmaids are far too young for us—all those who were young with us have moved away from these fields; how have we come back here?
> “You are so strange, Kristin,” whispered Erlend during the dance. “I grow afraid of you, Kristin—are you not glad—”
> They went from house to house and greeted their guests. Many candles burned in every room, and they were full of people drinking, singing, and dancing everywhere. Kristin felt she no longer knew her own home—and she had lost all sense of time—the moments and images floated strangely, torn loose from one another.
> The autumn night was mild; musicians were in the yard too, and people danced around the bonfire. They cried that the bride and bridegroom must honor them too, and Kristin danced with Erlend upon the cold, dew-wet yard. It was as though this awakened her a little and cleared her head.
> Out in the darkness a bright ribbon of mist floated above the river’s murmur. The mountains stood coal-black against the star-strewn air.
> Erlend led her out of the dance—pressed her against himself in the darkness beneath a gallery.
> “I have not even had the chance to tell you—you are so fair, so fair and lovely. Your cheeks are as red as flames—” He pressed his cheek against hers as he spoke. “Kristin, what is the matter with you—?”
> “I am so tired, so tired,” she whispered back.
> “Soon we shall go and sleep,” answered the bridegroom, looking up toward the sky. The Milky Way had turned and ran almost due south and north. “Do you remember that we have not slept together since that one night I was with you in your storehouse at Skog—”
> A while later Sira Eirik called across the yard that it was now Monday. The wives came to lead the bride to bed—Kristin was so tired she could scarcely resist them as she ought for seemliness’ sake. She let Lady Aashild and Gyrid of Skog take and lead her out of the loft. The groomsmen stood at the foot of the stairs with burning tapers and drawn swords; they formed a ring around the women and accompanied Kristin across the yard and up into the old loft.
> The wives removed her bridal finery piece by piece and put it away. Kristin saw that over the foot of the bed hung the violet-blue velvet gown she was to wear the next day, and above it lay a long, snow-white, finely pleated linen cloth. It was the wife’s linen Erlend had brought her; tomorrow she would bind her hair in a knot and fasten it over it. It looked so fresh, cool, and soothing.
> At last she stood before the bridal bed on bare feet, with bare arms, dressed only in the ankle-length golden-yellow silk shift. They had set the crown upon her again—the bridegroom was to take it off when the two were alone.
> Ragnfrid laid her hands on her daughter’s shoulders and kissed her cheek—her mother’s face and hands were strangely cold, but it was as though weeping strained deep within her breast. Then she opened the bed and bade the bride sit down in it. Kristin obeyed and leaned back against the piled silk cushions at the headboard—she had to incline her head slightly forward to keep the crown in place. Lady Aashild covered her to the waist, placed her hands on the silk coverlet, took the bride’s shining hair, and spread it forward over her breast and bare, slender upper arms.
> The men led the bridegroom into the loft. Munan Baardsøn unbuckled Erlend’s gold belt and sword—when he hung it on the wall above the bed, he whispered something to the bride—Kristin did not understand what he said, but smiled as well as she could.
> The groomsmen unlaced Erlend’s silk garment and lifted the heavy, long piece over his head. He sat in the armchair, and they helped him remove his spurs and boots.
> Only once did the bride dare look up and meet his eyes.
> Then the good nights began. The loft emptied of wedding guests. Last of all Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn went out and closed the door of the bridal house.
> Erlend rose, pulled off his underclothes, and flung them onto the bench. He stood before the bed, removed the crown and silk bands from her hair, and laid them on the table. Then he came back and climbed into bed. Kneeling on the bed beside her, he put his hands around her head and pressed it against his naked, hot breast while he kissed her forehead all along the red mark the crown had made.
> She threw her arms around his shoulders and sobbed aloud—sweetly and wildly she felt that now it vanished: the fear, the ghostly visions—now, now once again there were only he and she. He lifted her face for a moment, looked down into it, and passed a hand over her face and body, strangely swift and rough, as though tearing away a covering:
> “Forget,” he begged, whispering hotly, “forget everything, my Kristin—everything except that you are my own wife, and I am your own husband—”
> With his hand he struck out the last flame and cast himself down beside her in the darkness; he too sobbed:
> “I never believed it, never in all these years, that we should live to see this day—”
> Outside in the yard the clamor gradually fell silent. Weary from the day’s ride and dizzy with drink, the guests wandered about a little longer for decency’s sake—but more and more slipped away and found the places where they were to sleep.
> Ragnfrid accompanied those most deserving of honor to their places and bade them good night. The master of the house, who should have helped her with this, she saw nowhere.
> Only a few small groups of young people—mostly servants—remained in the dark yard when at last she slipped out to find her husband and take him to bed. She had seen that Lavrans had become very drunk during the evening.
> At last she stumbled over him as she searched outside the storehouse yard—he lay facedown in the grass behind the bathhouse.
> Feeling in the darkness, she recognized him—yes, it was he. She thought he was sleeping, took him by the shoulder, and tried to raise him from the ice-cold ground. But he was not asleep—not entirely, at least.
> “What do you want?” he asked, his speech thick.
> “You cannot lie here,” said his wife. She supported him, for he stood swaying. With one hand she brushed down his velvet garment. “It is time for us to go to bed too, husband.” She took him beneath the arm and led the staggering man toward the farm; they went behind the buildings of the storehouse yard.
> “You did not look up, Ragnfrid, when you sat in the bridal bed beneath the crown,” he said as before. “Our daughter—she was less bashful; she was not bashful in her eyes when she looked at her bridegroom.”
> “She has waited for him seven half-years,” said the mother softly. “Then surely she might dare look up—”
> “The devil they have waited,” screamed the father, and his wife hushed him in fear.
> They were in the narrow passage between the back of the privy and a rail fence. Lavrans struck his clenched fist against the bottom beam of the privy.
> “I laid you here for mockery and shame, you beam. I laid you here so filth might eat you up. I laid you here as punishment because you struck down my little fair maiden. I should have laid you over my loft door and honored and thanked you with fine carving because you saved her from shame and sorrow—because you caused my Ulvhild to die an innocent child—”
> He turned, staggered against the rail fence, and fell forward upon it; with his head down upon his arms, he wept uncontrollably, with long, deep groans between.
> His wife put her arms around his shoulders:
> “Lavrans, Lavrans.” But she could not quiet him. “Husband.”
> “Oh, never, never, never should I have given her to that man. God help me—I must have known it all along—he had broken down her youth and her fair honor. I did not believe it—no, should I believe such a thing of Kristin—but I knew it nevertheless. Yet she is too good for that weak boy who has squandered himself and her—had he enticed her ten times, I should not have given her to him, so that he might waste more of her life and happiness—”
> “What other course was there?” said the mother despairingly. “You understand it too: she was already his—”
> “Yes, I need not have made so great a commotion to give Erlend what he had already taken for himself,” said Lavrans. “A noble husband my Kristin has gained—.” He tore at the rail fence. Then he wept again. Ragnfrid thought he had grown somewhat more sober, but now the drunkenness overcame him again.
> As drunk and beside himself with despair as the man was, she did not think she could take him up to the hearth-room where they were to sleep—it was full of guests. She looked around—nearby stood a small barn where they stored the good hay for the horses during spring work. She went over and peered inside—no one lay there; then she led her husband in and closed the door behind them.
> Ragnfrid raked hay around herself and him and covered the outside with their cloaks. Lavrans wept from time to time and said something, but it was so confused she could find no meaning in it. After a while she lifted his head into her lap.
> “Dear husband—when they have bestowed such love upon each other—perhaps everything will be better than we think—”
> Lavrans answered in broken phrases—he now seemed clearer again:
> “Do you not understand—he now has complete power over her, he who has never been able to govern himself. It will be hard for her to find courage to oppose anything her husband wishes—and if one day she must do so, then she herself will suffer so bitterly from it—my gentle child—.
> “Now I scarcely understand any longer why God lays so many heavy sorrows upon me—I have labored faithfully to do his will. Why has he taken our children from us, Ragnfrid, one after another—first our sons, then little Ulvhild, and now I have given the one I held dearest, without honor, to an unsafe and foolish man. Now we have only the little one left, and it seems unwise to rejoice over her until I see how matters go with Ramborg.”
> Ragnfrid trembled like a leaf. Then her husband put an arm around her shoulder:
> “Lie down,” he asked, “and we shall sleep—” and with his head against his wife’s arm he lay for a while, sighing now and then, but at last fell asleep.
> It was still pitch-dark in the barn when Ragnfrid stirred—she wondered that she had slept. She felt before her with her hand; Lavrans sat upright with his hands clasped around his knees.
> “Are you awake already?” she asked in surprise. “Are you cold?”
> “No,” he answered, his voice rough, “but I cannot fall asleep again.”
> “Is it Kristin you are thinking of?” asked the mother. “It may go better than we think, Lavrans,” she said again.
> “Yes, that is what I am thinking of,” said the man. “Yes, yes. Maiden or wife, at least she entered the bridal bed with the one upon whom she had bestowed her love. Neither you nor I did so, my poor Ragnfrid.”
> His wife gave a dull, deep groan—she flung herself on her side in the hay. Lavrans moved a hand to her shoulder.
> “But I could not,” he said passionately and painfully. “No, I could not be—as you wanted me to be—when we were young. I am not like that—”
> After a while Ragnfrid said softly, weeping:
> “We have lived well together nevertheless—Lavrans—all these years.”
> “So I myself believed,” he answered darkly.
> Thoughts tumbled and crowded within him. That one naked glance in which bridegroom and bride had looked at each other, the two young faces flaring into red flame—to him it seemed shamelessness. It had scorched him; this was his daughter. But he continued to see those eyes—and wildly and blindly he struggled against the veil being torn from something in his own heart whose presence he had never wished to know, which he had defended against his own wife when she sought it—.
> He had not been able, he insisted harshly to himself. In the devil’s name, he had been married off as a boy; he had not chosen for himself; she was older than he—he had not desired her; he had not wanted to learn this from her—love. He still grew hot with shame when he thought of it—that she had wished him to love her when he had not wanted such love from her. That she had offered him all this which he had never asked for.
> He had been a good husband to her, so he himself had believed. He had shown her all the honor he could, given her full authority beside him and consulted her in everything, been faithful to her—and six children they had had. He had merely wanted to be allowed to live with her without her always grasping after that in his heart which he himself would not lay bare—.
> He had loved no one.—Ingunn, Karl’s wife at Bru. Lavrans blushed in the pitch darkness. He had always visited them when he traveled down through the valley. He had surely never even spoken with the mistress alone once. But when he saw her—when he merely thought of her—he sensed something like the first scent of the fields in spring, just after the snow has gone. He knew now that it could have happened to him too—he too could have loved.
> But he had been married so young, and he had become shy. Then he had come to thrive best out in the desolate forest—upon the uplands, where every living creature wishes for wide space around itself, room through which to flee—shyly they watch every stranger who wishes to steal close to them—.
> There was one time of year when the animals in the forest and mountains forgot their shyness. When they displayed themselves before their females. But he had been given his. And she had offered him everything for which he had not sued—.
> But the young in the nest.—They had been the small warm spot in the wilderness, the innermost sweetest delight in his life. The little fair heads of the girls beneath his hand.—
> Married—that he had been—almost without being asked. Friends—he had many, and he had none. War—that had been joy, but there was no more war—his armor hung in the loft, little used. He had become a farmer—. But he had had his daughters—everything he lived had become dear because through it he kept them safe, the delicate, soft little lives he had held in his hands. He remembered Kristin’s little two-year-old body upon his shoulder, her flax-pale, soft hair against his cheek. Her small hands holding his belt while she butted her round, hard child’s forehead between his shoulder blades when he rode out with her behind him on the horse.
> And now she had those same hot eyes—and she had obtained what was hers. She sat there in the half-shadow against the bed’s silk cushions. In the candlelight she was wholly golden—golden crown and golden shift and golden hair over the bare golden arms. Her eyes were no longer bashful—.
> Her father writhed with shame.
> And yet it was as though his heart burst open and bled. For what he himself had not received. And for the wife beside him to whom he had not been able to give it.
> Sick with compassion, he reached in the darkness for Ragnfrid’s hand:
> “Yes, I thought we lived well together,” he said. “I thought you grieved over our children.—Yes, and that you had a heavy disposition. I never thought that it might be I who was not a good husband to you—”
> Ragnfrid trembled as though in convulsions:
> “You were always a good husband, Lavrans.”
> “Hm.” Lavrans sat with his chin all the way down upon his knees. “Even so, perhaps you would have fared better if you had been married as our daughter was today—”
> Ragnfrid sprang up and cried low and piercingly:
> “You know! How did you find out—how long have you known—”
> “I do not know what you are speaking of,” said Lavrans after a while, his voice strangely crushed.
> “I am speaking of this: I was not a maiden when I became your wife,” answered Ragnfrid, and her voice was clear and ringing with despair.
> After a while Lavrans answered as before:
> “I never knew that before now.”
> Ragnfrid lay down in the hay, shaken by weeping. When the fit had passed, she lifted her head a little. A little gray light began to seep through the opening in the wall. She could make out her husband sitting with his arms twined around his knees, motionless as though he were stone.
> “Lavrans—speak to me—” she lamented.
> “What do you want me to say?” he asked, unmoving.
> “Oh, I do not know—that you should curse me—strike me—”
> “It would be somewhat late now,” answered the man; there was something like the shadow of a mocking smile in his voice.
> Ragnfrid wept again. “Yes, I did not think of how I betrayed you. I felt I myself had been so betrayed and so violated. No one had spared me. They brought you—I had seen you only three times before we were married—I thought you were merely a boy, red and white—so young and childlike—”
> “I was,” said Lavrans, and a little more resonance entered his voice. “And therefore I would have thought that you, who were a woman, would be more afraid to—to betray one who was so young that he understood nothing—”
> “That was how I came to think afterward,” said Ragnfrid, weeping. “When I had come to know you. Soon enough the time came when I would have given my soul twenty times over if I could have become guiltless toward you.”
> Lavrans sat silent and motionless; then his wife said again:
> “You ask nothing?”
> “What use would it be? It was he whose—funeral procession we met on Feginsbrekka when we carried Ulvhild into Nidaros—”
> “Yes,” said Ragnfrid. “We had to leave the road—go into the meadow. I watched them carry his bier past—with priests and monks and armed servants. I heard he had obtained a good death—reconciled with God. I prayed, as we stood with Ulvhild’s litter between us, that my sin and sorrow might be laid at his feet on the last day—”
> “Yes, you likely did,” said Lavrans, and the same shadow of mockery lay in his quiet voice.
> “You do not know everything,” said Ragnfrid, cold with despair. “Do you remember that he came out to us at Skog during the first winter we were married—”
> “Yes,” answered the man.
> “When Bjørgulf struggled with death—. Oh, no one had spared me. He was drunk when he did it to me—afterward he said he had never loved me; he did not want me—he asked me to forget it—. My father did not know this; he did not betray you, you must never believe that. But Trond—we were each other’s dearest friends then; I complained of it to him. He meant to threaten that man into marrying me—but he was only a boy, and he was beaten—. Afterward he advised me to keep silent and take you—”
> She sat still for a while.
> “When he came out to Skog—. A year had passed; I did not think so much of it. But he came there—he said he repented; he would have had me then if I had been unmarried—he loved me. So he said. God must judge whether he spoke truth. When he had left—I dared not go onto the fjord; I dared not because of the sin, not with the child. And then I had—then I had begun to love you so!” She screamed once, as though in the wildest torment. The man quickly turned his head toward her.
> “When Bjørgulf was born—oh, I thought I loved him more than my life. When he lay struggling with death—I thought, if he is extinguished, then I too am extinguished. But I did not ask God to spare the boy’s life—”
> Lavrans sat a very long time before asking, dead and heavy:
> “Was it because I was not his father?”
> “I did not know whether you were,” said Ragnfrid, stiffening.
> For a long time both sat deathly still. Then the man suddenly asked vehemently:
> “In Jesus’ name, Ragnfrid—why do you tell me this—now?”
> “Oh, I do not know.” She wrung her hands until the finger joints cracked. “So that you may take revenge upon me. Drive me from your farm—”
> “Do you think that would help me—?” His voice trembled with mockery. “And then there are our daughters,” he said quietly. “Kristin—and the little one.”
> Ragnfrid sat for a while.
> “I remember how you judged Erlend Nikulaussøn,” she said softly. “What judgment, then, do you pass upon me—?”
> A long shudder ran through the man’s body—released some of the rigidity.
> “You have now—we have now lived together for twenty-seven years—nearly. It is not as with a stranger. I understand that you have had it worse than hard.”
> Ragnfrid collapsed sobbing beneath his words. She dared reach for one of his hands. He did not move, but sat still as a dead man. Then she wept louder and louder—her husband sat equally motionless, looking toward the gray brightness around the door. At last she lay as though all tears had drained from her.
> Then he stroked fleetingly down her arm. She began to weep again.
> “Do you remember,” she said between her tears, “the man who once came to us while we were at Skog? The one who knew the old lays? Do you remember one about a dead man who had returned from the world of torment and told his son tidings of what he had seen? Then a din was heard from hell’s deepest ground: faithless women ground earth for food for their husbands. Bloody were the stones they turned; bloody hung their hearts out of their breasts—”
> Lavrans said nothing.
> “I have thought of those words throughout all these years,” said Ragnfrid. “Every day it was as though my heart bled, for every day it seemed to me that I ground earth for your food—”
> Lavrans did not himself know why he answered as he did. He felt empty and hollow in the breast like a man upon whose back they have carved the blood eagle. But he laid his hand heavily and wearily upon his wife’s head and said:
> “Earth must surely be ground, my Ragnfrid, before food can grow.”
> When she tried to take his hand and kiss it, he tore it violently away. Then he looked down at his wife, took one of her hands and laid it upon his knee, and bowed his cold, stiffened face toward it. And thus they remained sitting, without moving and without speaking further together.
